Author of "The Iron Pirate " "The s," "A Puritan's Wife,"

CHAPTER XXXVII. (Continued).

lin which We hear of Henry Gastonard at the Pavilion Henry Quatre, in the town of St. Germain by Paris.]

It would be impossible, Paddy, to tell you of my owns sensations during those instants of waiting. •Depict me standing in tile' miserable patch of formal garden, at the door of a paltry red brick viha, listening, as I have never listened in all my life, tremb- ling, I do believe, in the very excitement of my hope. More than once the temptation to cry out almost overpowered inc. I must tell her that I had come—must let Mimi know that 1 waited for her. I cothu have beaten : down twenty doors in my rage against delay, smashed the glass of the window to atoms, and razed the very building to the ground. -Upon the oth2r side was this imperturbable Farman, as quiet, as cat-like as ever, listen- ing with bent ear, betraying no emotion; seemingly convinced already of his success. Abe I must obey him faithfully, wait as he waited, crush my impatience in hands of iron. On, I say, it was intolerable, and yet it was the truth !

No one answered to our bold knock; the silence, became almost insupportable. A mindte we ' waited, two minutes, and still there was no sound bat that of our own quick breathing. As for the lamp which burned so brightly, we could see it plainly, standing upon the table of the front room and the single ornament of that bare apartment. For the rest, there was no carpet on the floor, no ornament, no picture—but just the roam it- self and the bare wooden table and the lamp standing upon it. • This we might have looked for, but not for the mystery of the Silenee, -ithe absolute stillnoos 'which met 'us—so that one could have heard a watch ticking in the hand. Were the men warned, then? Had they fled the place? My heart sank low at the thought—and yet it waa a thought that crept upon me. had spoken no word to Farman since we entered the garden of the house, but this new turn was not to be bor.le, and I could suffer it no longer. A hurried whisper asked him what he made of it—and, a little to my sur- prise, he answered me aloud. "They are asleep," he said quietly; "we must, wake them"—and he knocked so loudly that the hound began to bay again, and could hear the voice of Oleander cursing him. Plainly, we had no further need of c'onceal- meat.

"Who is asleep? " I. asked a little brutally. "Did you not te:l me that Madame Ga3tonard was her 2?"

"I -believed so," he . answered as quietly. "'You believed so—w2ll? "

"I shall tell you presently." His answer told me that he, with all his discernment, could make liNe of the Aitua- tion. My own advie2 had been is) force tie window of the room, ' and thi3 he now pro- ceeded to do—but first he lighted a :ittle lantern and laid his pistol on the sill. A disingenuous catch gave way at the first at- tempt, and we climbed through immediately, and went straight toward the inner door. Here for an instant Farman stood irresolute. "There may be some danger," he said— and then he asked me—" ai.e you quite pre- pared?" . _ _

I vhispered that I was, and he flung the door wide open, searching the hall beyond with the faint rays from a policeman's lantern. There were signs of habitation here such as we might ha ve expected—a felt hat upon a cane-seated chair, a basket such as women take to market, a stick so heavy that it was almost a bludgepn, an old mackintosh hanging upon a nail driven into the wall. Th.: floor was uncarpeted and showed mud from clumsy boots—at the far end the door of the kitchen stood open, and a flicker of firelight from the grate still flashed upon its plastered walls. Thither now we went cautiously. But the place was tenantless— though a kettle still sang upon the hob and some dishes stood unwashed upon the table. I often think, Paddy, that nothing is so sure a test of a man's nerves as a house of

unknown perils, which he must search room by room. I am afraid of little in this world. It is no mere boast—for these things are purely physical—but I possess some presence of mind beyond ordinary, and a contempt for many of the situations of danger which tradi- tion has glorified. And yet I swear to you, the sweat ran down my face like rain while I stood by Farman's side in that shabby kitchen and asked him, what next

No longer did I believe that Mimi was here—and yet I was forbidden to say that she was not here. The evidenca of recent occupa- tion, the shreds of coarse focd, the empty bottles lying pcl:-mell in the scullery, a woman's tattered bonnet flung to a corner, a little jug of milk set apart with a few dry -biscuits—these were the witnesses to Far- man's good faith and witnesses no logic could

shake. - As he had Spoken, so the truth—that my dear wife had been the captive of these rd.- flans in this very house, that she might even be a captive still or worse than a captive. For now I shall tell you that an overmaster- ing fear of the worst took pcssession of me and would not be quieted. I cared nothing for the men or the danger of their presence. Every step, long dragged out and heavy, Was as a step toward a dreadful seerct. The upper stories of the house became in an in- stant the chambers of the terrible truth. And above all was the torture of the thought that we had come too late, and but for these useless hours at St. Germain - might have saved her. This latter brought me to the nadir of despair. Even Farman took pity upon me. . "I begin to think that Madame is not here," he said quietly. "Let us go upstairs —we shall not be long in doubt." • I looked him full in the face, and did not spare him the question. ' "Is she alive, Farman?" "Why should they kill her? The black mailer never kills—he has not the .courage." I could but shrug my shoulders. "Then their object has been blackmail?" "It could be nothing else, Monsieur." I admitted his reasoning, but it did little console me. If there were peril of our proceeding this must be the :foment of it. For we had to climb the narrow stairway, ignorant of those who were above, and. powerlessi to shield ourselves from their at- tack. How it caMe, that I was up on the first floor before Jules Farman I am not able to tell you. I remember only that I stood on a dark landing listening to my own heavy breathing, and unable to distinguish other sounds. What light there was came astreak through a. narrow window high above us. I eould make out the shapes of doors, but they -were shut and meaningless. The floor was but a black patch until a warm ray of light shone down upon it from my companions Lantern and instantly declared its secret. An old woman lay there—a 'shrivelled, white-faced hag of a woman, whose clothes were little more than a bundle -of rags, whose hand still clutched the heavy stick with which, perchance, she had been struck down. And this Jezebel had gone to her ac- count. The mask of death is sometimes un- mistakable. It was unmistakable on Friday night when 'I came face ,to face with the old woman, Marie of Orleans, upon the landing nf the house at Bougival. I say that it was a dreadful discovery, and yet, God knows, mY thoughts in the instant M it were les of this stricken huddled body upon the floor than of the events which had preceded the mwder. , There is always awe nf death. .Paddy, however humble the sub- ject, however callous the discoverer. And at

the dark of the night in a lonely house wiUj mystery whispering all about, the awe manifold. Here we were, stooping to pnt our hands upon the dead woman's heart, listening as we did so for any sounds from the secret rooms, and yet; perchance think- ing of our own safety all the While.. • Wbe had been the instruments of our vengeance unon this mumbling hag? Must we unearth them presently, strike them down as thcy had struck her, spend the precious hours in such a butcher's task?

For my part, I thought that any instant might brirg the ruffians upon us. It was a trial intolerable to watch the closed doors and wait for them to open. Why did the men delay? And Mimi—my God; why was she silent ? Then a better ip-o..inct began to say that she was not here, and this gave me courocre. Let Inc know the tact for a truth, and I cared not how many villains were har- boured here.

We opened the doors one by one, Farman carrying his lantern. I had a revolver at the cock. Bat I shall tell yon at once that we discovered nothing. Thero were bads in two of the rooms, and a third had a pitry amemblement which spoke of a gentler oc.mpa tion

But in the -main the &use remained :he same hard and chilling villa that we had niagined it to be—and, I vow that there was something beyond all words melancholy in that secret which lay at the heart of it Au empty, barren house and a dead womas body upon the stairs. So much for Boni- val—so much for all our plotting and our .planning and our bold emprise. The men who had done this thing had been warned. Tlicy had fled' the neighbourhood, cheated us, and perchance the police. Ev Farman admitted as much when he called no together and deigned, for the first time, to share a confidence. ,

"Gentlemen," he said, "I cannot blame myself. The man Bedott3 was here an hour ago—l knew that Madame Gastonard wa;

h-ere. at sunset. You see what has happened —there has been a quarrel, and this woman has been killed. I would have. come to Bougival sooner if it had been safe to come— but I Was afraid for Alndame Gastonard while the showman Gondre wag here. We set a trap for him and he has been tnken at Asnieres to-night. The other man, Bedotte,

has. not been sober for many days. That is I,vhy I came to the house as I aid; but, •be- lieve me, if what I surmise be true, .nothing has been lost by delay, and we shall have good news of Madame to-morrow. I am now to leave the police to do their own busines:, here and to advise Monsieur Lepine of ours: We may return immediately to Paris, for our work ia done."

And so, Paddy, we left this - melancholy house and returned to the car. I can still see the villa, the lamp shining from the lonely room, and the river bathed in moon- light—for the moon was up by this time and all the scene made glorious. It was some- thing, at least, to know that my beloved wife had escaped that mean temple of death, per- chance had known nothing of its secrets. None the less, I ching to the neighbourhood as to some place which should minister to my sentiment, and, determining to stay the night' at St. Germain, returned tinther with my companions. They, be sure, were not the men to decline such hospitality, and they sat op with me until dawn, offering a thousand explanations of Farman's conduct, and justl iyinc, it in no way. What was this man keeping back from. us`: Why did he, who had served me so faithfully many a day, serve me so ill to-night? Recol- lect that I had but the shabb.est of facts from him. He had told me merely that my wife had been abducted from her house by those who had known her as Mimi La God--

iche at Montmartre, and who believed that they &mid profit by the knowledge. And upon this a talk of blackmail—y2t not a word that would enlighten me, no names, no his- tories—nothing but the intimation. Th:s, we said again and again as we sat in my room at the Pavilion Hotel and waitecl for the clay. Circumstance had d2luded us. We could make nothing of it.

I had nothing from Farman yesterday, but to-day there came a little note in which. evading other issues, he tells me that the man Bedotte has been traced to Rheinis, and is evidently making for Brussel3 ; 'but that the pollee are close upon his track, and an immediate arrest is expected. "As for Madame," he soya, "the opin'on is growing that she escaped from the house and need no longer be sought among these people." But of this he will write me further at a later hour. '

And so you see, Paddy, that I am • tied to this hotel in as great a state of doubt and perplexity, of hope and longing, as ever mor- tal suffered.- I know not what to decide, what 'to believe. Inconceivable, indeed, that Mimi should not have gone straight to Paris. if this tale of escape were true. A telegram assures me that nothing is known of her. either at the Hotel St. Paul or at Mont- martre; and had you in England any news," I doubt not it would have come to me before this. What, then, am Ito say? That slic has not wished to return to me? God forbid any such thought. I will send you another letter in the morn- ing, as soon as the . event permits. Should anything happen in London, let nothing delay a telegram. Of the trivial affairs, there is a request here from the editor of the "Daily Bulletin " that I will write a second letter for him. It •would serve no purpose, and I have said so. His desire to see me privately dictates the wish that you shall be my ambas- sador. Quit the game of golf and the per- ambulators end spend a quiet hour in Fleet- street. The power of the press is a wonder- ful thing, I assure you. but the journalist at lunch by no means terrifying. Ask the good fellow to meet you at the Savoy, and I do not think the state of parties will forbid. _ .

How -odd it seems to be writing like this. I feel it not at all. . The shadows crowd upon me. If I Could but say, Let there be light ! Yours, dcur Paddy,

HARRY GASTONAED,

Well, my dear, I am -having the blazes of a time in this wilderness of a Once, and all tor my friend's sake; though, God knows .what use 1 am to him any more than the police- Man at tile corner, who has had many a goed glass of my whisky, and wbuld like many another. Harry says 'tis to Paris I am to go presently, though what for the old geni:ie- man himself would be hard put to it to guess. The last news I have from him speaks of dreadful things we read in the papers this morning, it would be clear that the little witch is gone from the people that have had charge of her, and that this wicked story of Wrong and mystery is no clearer to us than ever it was. But so far as it goes, we must be content with it; for I would no more doubt her than I would doubt my sister Clara, and whatever she has done has been done for the best—of that 1 am sure.

Did' it never occur to you that this pretty child may have 'a history out of the ordinary. It has been in my mind since the first da. of our meeting, and is more in my mind than ever to-day. NV ho was her father? but, more important to ask, what was her mother's name? Did you never hear tell of the airs and graces of her, the pretty ways that were strange in a showman's tent, and the dignity which no man ever humbled? We may nave lost good manners in this twentieth century, Mts. Warrington, but we haven't lost tie good smse which tells us whether ohr fathers were gentlemen or villains, and this is an instinct we'll keep yet awhile. I say that Mimi Gastonard is the daughter neither of a showman nor a peasant, And if my surmise is not correct, put Paddy O'Con- nell down with the fools. To speak of things better understood, I don't wonder to hear that • you were annoyed about the horse-racing. 'Tis no consolation to have missed those same great races, the CEesarewitch and Cambridgeshire, and you so near to the course. Your cousin John evidently knows a good thing, and his win upon the double event must have gladdened your heart. But I'm sorry to hear that he put but a sovereign apiece oil for you, and he might well have made it a termer. Man' is a curious animal, and always niggardly about his own kills. I shall tell Mr. John that same if ever I meet him.

Well, Martha, I miss the piquet we used to play on quiet afternoons, and that's a cer- tainty. This god-forsaken Hampstead puts pistols in my hands every evening, and takes them out again when the sun shines in the mornmo • (7. Just to think that the riding has begun in Ireland, and me, Paddy O'Connell, doomed to a six-shilling hack and a gallop as far as your arms can reach. I told him a story or two about the parish priest of Glendalough, who, when the Bishop told him that golf was sending men to the devil fast, replied that he wondered at it, for they did it mostly on sloe gin. After this, ne asked me to write a series of papers on "a humorist in the mountains of Ir2land." But I declined immediately. "'Twould be over the heads of your people," says I, "and that's where all good Catholics should be in this life or the next."

I expect to gob; Paris to-morrow or the day after, and will write you when I get there. There is a parcel of books at the house, sent to you by your husband; but you don't seem to have opened them. Will I for- ward them on or give them to the heathen? Advise me by return.

And with kind regards, please find .me, yours, as per last, PADDY O' CON NELL. There was a curate man got hold of me in Hampstead, and took me to a Christian En. d?avour meeting. He persuaded Me to put .on the boxing gloves, and one of his' flock gave me a precious black eye. "I'was a Christian endeavour surely, and cost m, a bandage. So I'm only seeing half of this letter, which you can tell your husband if it should fall into his hands.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The King and Queen.—One notices with pleasure that the King and Queen, retreshed by their holidays, have been able to rease.various engagements, not only in London but in the provinces. Their 41ajesties usually spend Whitsuntide at Windsor Castle, but this year an exception \VHS made, and the King end Queen paid a three days' visit to Lord and Lady Allington at Crichel—their beautiful place in Dorsetshire. The Court will be at %%indsor for Ascot race-week, June 14-19, shen theie is to be a great military ceremonial in the Home Park, and the usual Ascot royal garden party at the Castle. But it is not only to the social world that Royalty gives a touch of brilliance. Their Majesties are to pay public visits to Manchester and Birmingham early in July, and scarcely a day passes that we do not find one or other member of the Royal Family devoting a good part of his or her time in helping forward some good work. The Queen at the Tournament.—Anyone would be blind indeed who did not recognise that our King and Ro3al Family have merits of their own which fully account for the undoubted popularity which they enjoy. But perhaps the thing that appeals to us most, is their many acts of thcughtfullindeess to individuals, or on behalf of some worthy cause, of which we find an . example in the Queen's presenceat the Military Tourna- ment, in order to aid the naval and military charities, although this necessitated the abandonment of her arrangements to accompany the King to Epsom to witness the race for the Oaks. her -Majesty was ascom- panied by Princess Victoria and her, grandchildren, Princess Mary of Wales and her two younger brothers, who !sere immensely interested in the display. • The Flourishing Florists.—The Temple Flower Show is always patronised as one of the society functions of the London season and reminds one that few businesses can be more flourishing ts an that of the high-class florist. Normerly the society woman was content with a few flowers in • her draeing room. Now they fill all her rooms' and even find a place in her electric brougham, whilst she requites costly blooms and sprays for her y 3rsorml adornment—for wearing in her hats, corsage end hair. The old world method of flower decoration by great bunches of massed blooms Las quite gone out, and the fashionable florist now uses the choicest blooms singly or very sparingly for his tahle "loser schemes." ' The cost of these for an ordinary dinner party may be anything from £25 upwards, and in the three months London season, the hostess who entertains moderately may easily run up a florists bill of £7OO to £l,OOO or more. , The Democratic Age.—The Temple Gardens, where the Ro3al Horticultural Society holds its annual show, used to I e at one time the favourite resort of the Court ladit a and fashionable promenaders. Now they serve as an open-air luncheon resort and lounge for workers ssr wages. The plebian throng has in fact invaded all the one-time fashiuntible resoits of the aristocracy. Hyde Park used to have its fashionable hour, and its habitues were du ssed for the occasion, ‘‘hilst, the horses and carriages attiacted all attentie n by their magnificence. Nowadays embody goes there, dressed anyhow, or in any kind of vehicle, and the leisured and the loafers rub shoulders with another. Names in Romance.—Fiction writets will have a difficulty in naming their Ninths and aosenttireses if, as the law stands they are open to he sued for libel by people osnittgBiflhi rnmes. It this interpretation of -she law as to accidental libel is upheld, tne only safe way will be to give these characters algebraical symbols. This recalls the trouble which befel Mr. Pipets, who thought he was quite safe when he invented the title the " Notorious Mrs. Ebb-Smith." There are however, so many kiuds of Smiths, and there happened to be a real 111 re. Ebb-Smith, was unhappily committed suicide under the attain of her sudden notoriety. • Simplified Funerals.—lt is a matter for satisfaction that the old-time display and panoply of funerals, and of mourning attire, have greatly g, ne out of fashion. Charles Dickens eas elle of the pioneers in the crusade against extravagant display, and one seldom sees an unneces,ary parade nowadays, except it is the matter of flowers. The example of a wealthy lady who recently died, and denounced in her will the iolly of expensive funerals, was followed by the late Sir Matthew Gossett, and the late Eire of Leicester, it will he remembered limited his burial to the simplest degree. So also did the late Mr. Charles Mon icon, who is supposed to have amassed a fortune of ten or twelve millions of money. Now and again we see an exception but speaking gener- ally the days of n apnificent funerals are past and gone, and the reform is an evidence of public good taste. Whist Drives.—Tlae ordinary social %%hist drive is usually a very harmless and perhaps silly affair. No one would accuse its regular devotees of being reckless gamblers, hut Preheudary Webb-Peploe does not approve Of them and he says that "it is diseraceful that methods such as whist drives should he imperted into the work of religion." Much the same has often been said shout church bazaar raffles, which the police no longer allow and it is perhaps just. as well that they have somewl:at tardily stepped in to stop the public whist drives, organised for personal gain ; which have been a feature of the past winter sees-n. One is glad to think that with the good time of summer at hand, whist drives will taken back seat, althoughwe may not share Prebendary Webb-Peploe's puritan objections to the playing of any games of chance. The Bandeau Again.—Frim Paris comes the news that the bandeau is in contemplation, and that soon not only will the fashionable woman be able to make use of both es es when taking her walks abroad, but that plirnpses of her hair will be caught as the season advances. There is no getting ae ay from the fact that fruit and vegetables, mike generally, for vulgarity, and that we should all do well to leave such ornamentation(?) severely/ alone. ===== 0002601_19090605_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
[By Redcap.]

POULTRY FOR EXHIBITION.

(Continued,)

Generally speaking the ordinary methods of feeding are , sufficient, except at certain times, for special reasons. Several weeks before the Show the birds intended for exhibition should be separated from the rest of the stock, and kept in a small house or covered run. Then you can commence to get them into thoroughly good show, condition by special attention and careful feeding. To obtain an abundance of feather with a good gloss, it is necessary that you should feed specially to . produce it. and a valuable menu for that purpose is as follows : —Bread and milk for the morning meal, biscuit . meal. • (to which has been added flesh meat) for the mid-day meal, and a good sample of hard corn at nigtt, not forgetting to give 'green food of some kind. Might I_ also remind you to frequently change the drinking water, because the tendency of birds so kept in a Continued area is to scratch about in the pen and the 41tter gets into the water trough and contaminates it. In fact it is advisable, to take the water away after each meal and then thorough cleanliness in this direction is assured. These little hints may seem very 'unimportant to some cf my readers but you find that experience will soon te.ich. you that in the preparation of exhibition specimens every detail has its -own place in the scheme of things. As was pointed out last week the same methods of feeding sdft-feathered varieties will not do for the hard feathered tribe. More difficulty is experienced in getting the hard-feathered ones into good all-round condition than is the case with the others already mentioned. Because while you are trying to get hardness and closeness of leather you want at the same time to get the gloss or sheen on them, if the same feeding is resorted to as that recommended for the soft-feathered birds then you will not be successful. While you may and would get the gloss, you would at the same time have them too loose in feather. Therefore you see the need for special treatment according to the constitution of the variety you take in hand. I should recommend in preparing hard-feathered birds for show that instead of giving a mid-day meal of soft food that such should only be given for the morning feed and should, not consist of that recommended for the soft-feathered ones, but rather biscuit-meal, ane part flesh meat in comparison to the quantity being prepared, well dried off with middlings. Then at noon give a little green-food and some good sound red-wheat, and at night Indian corn. A regular supply of clean green food and the addition of animal food in the morning's meal will be sufficient in producing the gloss on the feathers. The key-note of success in exhibition birds' is condition. However well bred your birds .may be if shown in bad condition they will never do you much , winning. Careful attention in feeding and keeping them clean and in a healthy iiondition.while preparing for show is essential. The next item of importance is the

===== 0002601_19090605_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
FINAL PREPARATION

for the show 'bench. Let me first .of all remind you of the necessity for placing your bird in the show pen for the last few days prior to the show to make sure that they are thoroughly trained and perfectly docile and that they handle well. Give them "tit-bits," such • as scraps of meat, from your .hand and . hold smile from the top of the pen so that they have to reach for it. .This gets them into the habit of standing erect and show- ing themselves to "perfection." It is a good "wrinkle." I have often noticed that when you approach the pen of a bird so treated that directly you get near the pen it stands erect and quite naturally expects a little treat from the top of the pen. Many fanciers when visiting a show where they happen to be competing take a few scraps with them for their birds and, thus help to fix the habit of showing off well. The day before the event a. very special process has to be gone through, that of washing all over in the case of white birds, but simply the washing of legs, feet and face in the dark coloured oneo. Now to perfectly wash. and dry a white bird fit for keen competition is not at all an easy matter and needs to be carried out strictly according to directions. Before going into details, however, I would advise you to experiment on any white plumaged bird not fit for show purposes, for by so doing you are not only avoiding risks but getting yourself more competent. First then, get someone to assist you. Then get a tin bath such as is generally used for the " bairns fill three parts full of very warm water (not. above the natural heat of the birds) in which has been dissolved a piece of white- soap. Take • the bird gently but firmly and place in the water, and when thoroughly soaked rub the soap .well into the feathers until you think all dirt and stains are removed. Then empty the bath and place the bird again into clean warm water and rinse out all the soap from the feathers. Then thoroughly rinse again in lukewarm water to which has been added some ordinary washinr, blue; this will bring back the whiteness of the feather. After this process place the bird in an exhibition basket' before a good fire and constantly attend to the bird until quite dry. Then put it into a show pen free from draught on a bed of nice clean straw. Washing should be done at least 24 hours before the show so that the bird will have had time to recover from such a - tryinc, ordeal (for such it is). In the case of darltcoloureebirds as I have already pointed out it is only necessary to wash the legs, feet and face. The plumage can be got up by dressing for a few minutes each day with a soft silk. handkerchief. You will be surprised what a differ- ence this makes to the brightness of the 'feathers. After washing the feet, etc., rub over with a small sponge that has been soaked in olive oil ; but don't put too much on, just gently rub them over to give them sufficient lustre to compare with the sheen on the feather.- Before

DISPATCHING

tl the show give your birds as much Indian corn as they will eat. This is recommended because it is a food that takes long to digest and as the railway journey is often a long one the birds get hungry and restless and therefore they are 'apt to damage their condition and as a result also their prospects of winning. The question of feeding on their return is also one of great importance for the fact of having been in a warm place and handled perhaps many times is very trying indeed, so that it is essential_ that a good "pick-me-up". be given in the shape of a feed of 144 bread and milk, not too sloppy. This is greedily eaten and is a valuable tonic as well as a nourishing food. For any class or breed of fowl such a Meal (which need not be continued) after being. exhibited is very helpful in keeping them in condition. Before leaving the question of preparation for show I want to give my readers a note of warning against the practice. of -

===== 0002601_19090605_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
" FAKING "

thin.. which is often resorted to by skilful yet unscrupulous fanciers. I know that it seems hard not to yield to the temptation of drawing or plucking out a mis-marked feather which may just spoil an otherwise good specimen. But better be honest and show your bird in its natural garb than risk the chance of having the distasteful disqualification card placed on your pen, not to speak of the, eye of suspicion of other fanciers.

I. shall continue next week and give you some methods of " faking " and how to avoid same in preparing birds for show.

(To be con(inued).

===== 0002601_19090605_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COAL-MINING INDUSTRY AND FREE TRADE. .

Tariff Reformers described the removal of the export coal duty as the wanton abandonment of a considerable revenue (about £2,500,000 yer annum) which was wholly paid by the foreign consumers of the coal, and which was not perceptibly injurious to the owners of our coal mines nor the workers therein. -„

-.- ----- The assumption is very generally made, and seems justified, that a Parliamentary majority favourable to Tariff Reform would also be favourable to the reimposition of a coal export duty either at Is. per ton or some highf-r figure. It is noteworthy with regard to this question that Tariff Reforniers abandon altogether their usual. contention that a Tariff duty must be borne in part, and may be borne wholly by the producers of the commodity taxed. Yet if the c Mention be true of any competitively-produced commodity that a Tariff duty may be paid out of the' producer's profit margin. such contention would seem exceptionally plausible with regard to coal product ion. which i, in practical effect, a monopoly in the hands of ' the most powerfully organised and harmonious, bodies of employers and employed in the kingdom. Obviously these contradictory contentions cannot both be true.. If the tax were wholly paid by the foreign consumer, and if it were no tax upon or hindrance to coal production, then there would be nothing to depress coal prices at home. INCONSISTENT EXPECTATIONS.

Advocates of export duty on coal maintain : 1. That the .duty would be wholly paid by the foreign consumer, and therefore would not discourage the coal output. • • 2. That. 'i nevertheless, - a consequential effect would be the cheapening of Coal in some degree to the home consumer. Unquestionably this anticipa- tion is the cause of most of the popular support given to the proposed duty. -3. That our exported coal is an important aid to. foreign manufacturers, many of whom are thus, enabled to compete with British manufacturers much‘ better than they would be able to do if they' had not our -coal, or if they had to pay a tax thereupon. 4. It is urged that the total producing capacity of coalfields in this country is limited that within a century or two our best coalfields will be exhausted; and that the consequent rise in coal prices will dis- able this country from competing with foreign manu- facturers. _ Therefore it is contended that in the interests of our posterity we ought to discourage the exportation of coal as far as possible: • OPINION IN,THE COAL TRADE. - Coal owners and coal workers, who are surely id the. best position to 'know their own interests, were practi- cally unanimous in their opposition to the export tax: on coal; and unquestionably, their organised opposition, caused its repeal. Their arguments were 1. That the tax was very unequal and unjust in its- incidence. Most of the coal exported naturally goes from those mines which are closest to the seaports from which the coal can be shipped abroad. There- fore the pressure of the tax fell almost entirely upou these colleries ; whilst inland colleries did not feel the export tax at all.

2. That the tax on our export coal was indeed paid by the foreign consumer, especially in th, beginning, before the trade had adapted itself to th, new conditions. But on the other hand this extra shilling of cost to the foreign consumer afterward' perceptibly discouraged his demand for British coal. and stimulated competition from German and Belgian collieries, which were successful in obtaining large contracts for coal supplies that would otherwise have been met from British collieries.

3. That this reduced demand for the output of " collieries engaged in, aid hitherto largely dependent on, the exports trade, forced these latter to sell their - output competitively against the produce of our inland' collieries, thus lowering prices and profits for both. 4. That, altlitough to this extent there was a down— ward tendency in the prices paid by home consumers, the gain of these consumers was wholly at the expense - of a single section of the conummitsnamely,,,e_pal- owners and workers. These reasonably contended' that it was unjust and oppressive thus to confer a benefit upon home coal consumers by an artificial restriction of their foreign markets. Moreover, it was- " certain that the benefit to home consumers could only be temporary, inasmuch as the coal output must ill due course have been restricted proportionately to demand by the closing of the less profitable collieries. 5. The gain to foreign manufacturers by the use. of British coal is limited to the difference between the price of British coal and price of competing supplies from other sources. 6. For this gain, such as it is, to foreign manu- facturers, we have surely adequate compensation in the shape of the extra employment and resulting profits yielded by the export cod trade. Taking the• average of the five years 1903-7, 291 per cent, of all the coal raised from our mines was exported. There- fore, a stoppage of the exports would be stoppage of 291 per cent.- of our coal workers. That is, out of. the total of about 1,000,000 workers employed in our. mines, 300,000 would be thrown entirely idle ; thus depriving about 1,500,000 of the population of their livelihood. Beyond this there would be the great loss of British railway traffic in the 63,000,000 tons per annum of exported coal. True, an export tax of shilling per ton is far from being a prohibition Of. exports ; but the tax was, and would be again, a hindrance and an injury to the trade proportit nate to• the scale of the duty.

7. Far more serious than the railway losses, how— ever, would be the loss of freights to the British shipping, which carries abroad nearly the whole of these 63,000,000 tons of coal. Coal freights are the bulkiest Of all our exports—they fill, it is estimated, on the average, three-fourths of the total cargo-space in all our outward-bound vessels. Were it not for the coal exports, these vessel's would have trifling and unremunerative outward cargoes. Consequently ship- owners would be compelled to charge double freights on import cargoes. Several ltnillion tons of export coal, carried on long voyages to the East or to South America, earn much more in ship freightage than the. pit's-mouth value of the -coal itself. During the incidence of the coal export tax many shipowners' maintained that the effect of the tax was even more injurious to our shipping than to the coal producers. 8. As regards the limitation of our British coal-- fields and our duty to economise coal consumption for the, sake of our posterity, such a far-seeing econonV, supposing it to be our duty, is surely the duty of thc.:' whole nation, and not e burden that should be impose(t on a single industry or section of the nation. A present- day prosperity, consequent on the free use of our coalfields, which makes certain the I% ell-being of,o-ar children and grand-children, is incomparably more accord- with practical common sense than a foolisu endeavour to arrogate to the present-day generatlo3 supernatural powers of prophecy and direction as' the well-being of descendants max generations furthel removed.

The battleship Benbow, chiefly interesting as,beia the last of the group armed with 110-ton guns, is to b° offered for sde at Devonport next month. All the remaining vessels of the Admiral class are to be sold out of the set. vica betore the eud of the year, and Baltic fleet will then contain no battleships of an earlre' type than the Royal Sovereigns.

NOTES ON GAMES ETC.

Cricket.

-..Warrillgton enjoyed relaxation from League Cricket on Saturday when they entertained Boughton llall and for the second time this season. accounted for them in decisive fashion. 'rhe conditions militated against good batting, for the recent rains bad had their effect on the wicket and a cross wind also assisted the bowlers. Boughton's stay at the wicket lasted less than an hoar and realized.only 21 runs. 11. Callon und Cresswell were in great form with the ball, and came out with the fine figures of seven wickets for 13 runs, and two for nine respectively.

Warrington wiped off their opponents' score with only two wickets down but most of the batsmen could make little headway against the deliveries of Chu rton and Buckley, with the result that the Warrington aggregate reached only 63. W. H. Podmore (15) and W. Richardson (14) were the top scorers. Churton captured six wickets for 22 runs and Buckley three for 23.

The West Lancashire League contest has now got into full swing. On Saturday Prescot defeated St. Helens Recs. by 207 to 124 and Earlestown accounted for Newton by 107 to 60. In the second division St. Helens Rees. reversed the tables, Prescot being beaten by a margin of 39. Earlestown second easi y dis- posed of their Net on rivals, the respective totals being 176 and 53.

SECOND DIVISION.

St. Helens Rees Wallington Earlestowli Present St. Helens

Newton

In the W;irrington and District League Sankey \Vire Mills severely trounced the "Guardian " eleven. Batting first the Wire Mills made the nice round total of 50, and then tumbled out their opponents for seven. R. Brooks batted in fine style for his 22, and Bell and Bladen bowled very effectively.

On Monday morning Wire Mills 2nd met Bylauds's, but owing to the heavy rain the match had to be abandoned. Wire Mills declared their innings after amassing 79 for three wickets, S. L. Partridge dis- tinguishing himself with a splendid display for 41, whilst H. Hawthorn carried out his bat with 20 to his credit. flylands had mude only 13 runs with two wickets down when play ceased.

• In the Warrington and District Junior League Padgate triumphed at the expense of Rylands' 2nd, the majority being 19. Davey (18), Appleton (13) and Critchley (12) were the most successful batsmen.

Holy Trinity accounted for All Saints, with 38 to spare, M. Aukland being the highest scorer with 15. H. Fleming performed the hat trick.

Warrington 'Municipal Officers' Guild are having a most successful season and on Saturday they registered another victory at the expense of Rylands' Bros. For the winners D. Holland and S. J. Price batted in fine style for 17 and 14 respectively, while B. Green bowled with deadly effect, capturing five wickets for 20 runs.

There was a closely contested match between Warrington Thursday and " GuArdian " Office on Whit Monday, the printers running out winners by six runs. Not one player attained double figures.

Bold-street Wesleyans accomplished their first win of the season on Saturday, the Adult School being defeated by a majority of 32. Byrom (19), G. Bell (13) and P. Woods (11). batted well for the winners and A. Walker contributed 10 for the adult school.

Sankey Wire Mills entertained Urmston St. Clement's on Monday afternoon and disposed of them by a margin of 26. H. Hawthorn was in good form with the bat and compiled 28, D. Gray (13 not out) being the highst scorer for the visitors.

J. fiampson has been doing great things for Lymm this season and last week end he accomplished two fineiperformances. In the match again,t High Legh on 'Saturday he placed 53 to his credit, and, in the encounter with Birkenhead Municipal on Monday he carried out his bat after making 84. The Lymm eleven won both matches in easy style and H.S.Saville also did well with tho bat, making the totals of 21 and 37 (not out);

Strett:m and Appleton visited Penketh on Saturday and retutned vanquished by the Sankey eleven to the extent of 45. Foster, the home captain, did admirable service, knocking up 34 and taking five wickets for five 'ans.

I.,tchford Baptists had a little surprise at Arley on Monday, the I illagers tiiumphing over them by 31. J. V. Smith maintains his reputation as one of Arley's great assets and knocked up 32 not out. T. Dixon (15) was the top scorer for the Baptists.

Runcorn 2nd had little difficulty in afpounting for Moore and Dareshmy 11. Jones (22 not out) 'doing fine work Is ith the bat. S. -Taylor and the Rev. T. T. Lee Jones each made 11 for Daresbuy.

Bowling.

A keen match was witnes—sed in 'the Wati:ngton Works Competition on Saturday between the Firth Company (handicap JO) and Crosfield's No. (handi- cap 15), the latter only winning by 14 points., W. Stokes ((roAfield's) and T. Bolton . (Firth) played a good game, for the score kept on increasing in unison, and they were on a level at 13. However, Stokes got two more singles: and his ictory waa loudly applauded. Later the Firth Company's team had then- revenge. W. Lawton, sen. (Crosfield's) and T. Clare (Firth) were out together, and the former had got four, to his credit before the latter corld get one. Clare then put to his credit three points, IN. i th- out Lawton adding any With his next wood Lawton was' successful, but that .was all ha could get, and Clare gained a handsome victoty by 15-5. -

T. Taylor (Crosfield's) and T. Wilson (Firth) prc- Aided some excellent sport—that is, _towards the

finish of the encounter. The latter had amassed the the handsome score of 12 to his opponent's 4, when Taylor steadily worked himself up until they peeled at 14. Then came the trying time, but Taylor was victor by the odd point.

Greening's No. 1 and White Lead Works engaged in an interesting mateh. The former started with a handicap of 15. whilst the Lead Works had 25 to their credit before the match commenced. The play was fairly equal, the White Lead Works- only winning by 31 points. That the game between J. Green (Green- ings) and H. Rigby •(Lead Works) wrs a fine one, was agreed by all present, and details of the play will be of interest. Rigby obtained six, whilst his opponent could only register one, The game went - steadily- on until the figures were 10-9, Rigby having the lead. They peeled at 11 and also at 13. Rigby gained another one, but his opponent won the game.

The match between Orford Tannery No. 2 and Longford Wire, No. I was somewhat uneven, the latter wining by 67. points, although the former had a handicap of 30, while the latter had only 10. All the players of the Longford company gained the required figures, showing themselves to be by far the better te.mt. W. Simister, the captain of the Longford company, obtained 15, whilst his opponent (PA-Egli:lm) had to leave the green with a "duck." The keenest game was between T. Bibby (Orford) and G. .Clarke Aviv, played a very close game. They were equal at 7, but Bibhy got 4 more before Clarke obtained any more. In a little while the figures were 14 and 13 for Clarke and Bibl4y respectively, and then in the next wood Clarke g3t the other one needed.

The match between Whitecross No. 1 (scratch) and Wire Ropery (30) was one-sided, the former winning by :35 points. All the Whitecross men obtained 15, except Joe (Heave who placed 12 to his credit against Smallwood's 'l5. A. (Heave (captain of the winning team) beat his opponent J. Wilkinson by 15 to 1. The contest between Ted Cleave (Whitecross) and C. Perry was a very good one Gleave had 7 in hand before his Contestant could obtain any. When Perry got 1, his opponent's figures were 0, and there he rem:gined until Perry pheed 14 to his credit. However he did not get the coveted 15, for Weave with a fine effort won the game by 15-14.

Warrington bowlers have been to the fore in the Lancashire and Cheshire individual merit competition at Chorlton this week. In the first round A. Gleave, of Bull's Head, achieved a signal victory pver A. Poirette, of Eccles, by 21-0 and he was also successful in the second round. However; in the third round he came to grief against H. Arnold, of Didsbury. 'l'. Ellison, of Warrington Cemetery,last year's champion, did not survive the second round, his copquerer being H. Harrison, of Leigh Railway. J. Weave, of Bull's Head, is going strong, for in the third round he accounted for D. Clu,‘lter, jun., of Lloyds, by 21-14.

Swimming.

The switmning season is now in full swing, and during the Whit-week, holidays the schOol children have availed themselves of the opportunity of visitinn, the plunge baths in Legh-street. They are busily practising for the handicaps that frequently take place during the summer months.

The Warrington polo team are enthusiastically preparing themselves for the fray, and hope to have a fine score to their credit at the end of the season. If they maintain their present form the prospects are decidedly rosy.

• There was a practice by the polo team on Tuesday night there being a large number of members present. Some promising players were noted. Players for the Tuesday night match will be picked from the follow- ing, C. Woods, C. Turner, S. Ince, H. Woolstencioft, R. Pepper, T. Pemberton, W. Dunn and R. Beaman.

We are informed that R. Peers an old Warrington favourite who, at one time played for Warrington, will help Widnes.

Unfortunately for Warrington C. Ashton, will not play, for them this season owing to business reasons.

On Tuesday they will try conclusions with Widnes at Warrington. Last year the chemical town repre- sentatives gave Warrington a lively time but they should find doughty opponents on this occasion.

A squadron race between Widnes and Warrington will also be swum, and it is to be hoped there will be more powerful swimmers on the opposing side as the race between Runcorn and Warrington was little more than a farce.

A 50 yards members handicap should provide some excitement as the membership now includes some capital staying• swimmers. This handicap will include both junior and senior members.

A new feature has now come into vogue, that of season tickets. These tickets are available for memers of the club, and will entitle them to admission to all ordinary polo matches, also entrance to all junior members' races. These tickets are Is. each and may be obtained from Mr. Barlow at the baths.

APPLETON RIFLE CLUB.

A team of ten members of the above Club visited St. Helens on Saturday for the purpose of competing in a rifle shooting match with the St. Helens Civilian Club, whose range is situated on thc hill at Denton's-green. A strong wind prevailing made scoring very difficult for the visiting team, who get no practice in estimating windage owing to the sheltered position of their own range. The conditions were as follows :—Each team to consist of ten members, the best eight scores on each side to count; miniature rifles, any sights bar telescopic, ten shots at each of two ranges, 50 and 100 yards Decimal scoring. Highest possible at each range 100 per man or an aggregate of 200 for the two ranges. '1 he match resulted in a victory for the St. Helens Club who made a total of 1272 against 1221 by the visitors, the former thus winning by a majority of 49. It will be remembered that in a match fired on the same range under similar conditions between the two clubs last season the St. Helens Club won by a majority of 239 points, the • following being the scores on that occasion out of a hightest possible of 1680 :—St. Helens C.R., 1363 ; Appleten R.C.. 1125. We call it tl°- 15r advisedly; for not 0.0 is it evident that these aerial monsters Wle be made use of for military purposes befoc they are for commercial purposes, but it likely that they will never be of much C°l 1:1;t mercial use at all. An airship trying to a large steamer's cargo of granite or pig would look very silly. We would clear, too, that we are under no apprebell; sions as to Count ZEPPELIN'S bona fides, °it Germany's supposed desire to make a suddedo swoop upon us via the clouds. But we .0,1 hold that each advance that is made in aerl navigation does bring nearer what will b,eor major terror in war-time and a minor ter'do even in peace time, when aviators nothing worse than knock our chimneys cle drop mineral-water bottles on our heads, 3'

ruin the sunsets with their ugly craft. fit During the Whitsuntide holidays COlllB, ZEPPELIN beat all previous airship rec°l:, 0, He left his lair on Lake Constance I.'lect quarter to ten on Saturday night. his family in the van, swarmed out to ZEPPELINS supposed landing-place, prepared to sing the German equivalent of "See the Conquering Hero Comes." Unfortunately, no ZEPPELIN appeared. Either because the COUNT did not know his Monarch was expect- ing him (WILHELM 11. is a man it is incon- venient to disappoint), or because there was a strong head wind, the great ship turned, went south-west again over Weimar and tuttgart, and finished up at Goppingen, after travelling about a thousand miles in 381 hours. What lessons are to be drawn from the trip? The most obvious one is that this 'effort would easily have taken the Zeppelin 11. over the North Sea and back.' England's insular position, in fact,. is, from a military Point of view, diminished (we do not know by how much) in value. In any decently Moderate wind airships can , now travel for 'clays, answering the helm perfectly, and only ch'oPping when their human or combustible fuel is exhausted. It is said that when the:ship lauded the whole crew was tumbling about 'With sleepiness. That reminds one that air- hips to be of service in war will have, if possible, to be built to carry a double 'complement of men—provided it is feasible to sleep in the air. That means one 'More difficulty before the " conquest " of the heavens is really final. The bigger the weight, we imagine, the bigger (in the" dirigible " type) the gas-bag. Count Zeppelin's ship is an enormous size, and cost an enormous sum of money. His last big voyage ended in the total lestruction of Zeppelin 1., and Zeppelin J. very nearly came to grief over a pear-tree, 'Which would have tore the cumbrous vessel to pieces had the wind been stronger. We (1° not believe the future is to these huge dirigibles—they are too clumsy, too costly, O° unmanageable, and liable to damage on land. Even if Count ZEPPELIN girdled the globe he would be quite likely to lose his ship tt his next one-mile trip. Only when the 'cheap aeroplane is perfected will the world Teally know the worst that it is in for.

Ardent Tariff THE OPPOSITION Reformers have suffered TO THE FINANCE a disappointment from BILL. the decision of the

Opposition leaders to move the plain rejection of the Finance Bill. After so much loud proclamation of the failure of Free Trade finance, and of the need for a broadened basis of taxation, why should this manifest opportunity to proclaim the merits of Protection as an alternative be tamely thrown away.? But Mr. Balfour— and, for that matter, Mr. Austen Chamber- lain, ardent Tariff Reformer as he may be— is far too wary to wish to be drawn into a discussion of the alternative which would be necessary if the Liberal Budget were thrown out by the Lords. A statesman might feel almost overburdened with the responsibility of providing an adequate alternative under such circumstances, but a clever tactician gleefully recognises the immense advantage of simply moving that the measure "be read a second time upon this day six months." Why, upon such a motion the Opposition, which has an insatiate appetite for "Dreadnoughts," and introduced amendments to raise the cost of the Old Age Pensions Bill, can pose as a champion of economy, and Mr. Balfour, who has brought himself to accept a fiscal " reform" which would tax almost every imaginable com- modity in such a way that the consumer would have to pay more than the amount of tax upon it which the Treasury would receive, can froth over with declamation against the folly and the iniquity of the new Spirit Duties because it has been found impossible to prevent "the Trade". from raising its prices much above the level of the new tax.

Mr. Lloyd-George has THE LAND TAXES. wisely placed the new

land -taxes in the fore- front of the Finance Bill. The chief brunt of the attack will fall upon these taxes, and upon the liquor duties which immediately follow them. But it is also desirable that the land taxes should be. first cleared Out of the way, because they represent the most complex and far-reaching of the fiscal changes introduced by the Budget, and con- sequently it is in their case that the text of the Finance Bill has to step in to supplement the terms of the resolutions and the Chancellor's Budget speech. Not only is a new principle of taxation adopted in the case of land, but a new system of valuation is to be created. The Opposition organs talk as if there was some horribly sinister design behind this. To talk so is absurd. Those who have a good conscience do not shun inquiry. Mr. Lloyd- George has shown that he fully realises, in a way which must have come as a surprise to many representatives of rural " squire- archy," the obligations which fall upon the rural landowner to a degree quite strange to the best urban landowner. If they under- stand their own interest, rural landowners will welcome rather than shrink from the new quinquennial valuation. Some of the leading Opposition journals have been talk- ing about the new land taxes in a strain which can only be adequately described by a cant phrase of the journalistic profession. When they talk about Mr. Lloyd-George's animosity against the owner of agricultural land they have made a bloomer.' From the tax on undeveloped land agricultural land is exempt. Not only is a limit of £5O an acre fixed, which will easily exclude far the largest proportion of agricultural land, but special provision is made to meet the case of land above that limit where the site value of the land is due to its value for agricultural purposes.

There can be little doubt STATE SICKNESS that a scheme of INSURANCE, compulsory sickness and invalidity insur- ance would contribute materially towards the reduction of unemployment. Unemploy- merit appears to increase inversely upon itself. Such a tendency to rapid increase can only be laid under some check by means of reserve funds accumulated during pros- perity. They would provide automati- cally when it might be required an artificial stimulus to trade, since they would assist labourers out of work to obtain the necessaries of life. They would prevent, especially, a devastating poverty that demands costly recuperation if death is to be escaped. It is this risk of sickness and failing resources and the enfeeblement that follows which more than any needs a guard. A system of compulsory contributory State insurance theoretically would meet the need. But in practice such a system might be a dangerous failure, since it would attack the splendid enterprise that has built up and maintained the friendly societies, and might atrophy the individual desire and initiative to save. Mr. Gilchrist, an Oddfellow dele- gate, has detailed a seheme by which it is thought the State could work with the friendly societies and not overlap; but the scheme appears to present immense difficul- ties in application. For instanceithere is the casual labourer, who has obtruded himself so tiresomely into the practice of the Workmen's Compensation Act. How is he to be defined? He more than any kind of labourer needs assistance,but if he is paid by the day or by the hour how possibly is the proportionate contribution to be collected? What, too, is an employer? The pleasant compromise is not to be arrived at so simply. Mr. Churchill has promised to consider the problem with the assistance of the friendly societies, and to introduce legislation. The solution is, of course, too big to be attempted impulsively, and can only be reached by long experiment. Whatever scheme is introduced must; in our opinion, fully recognise the soundness of friendly society work, and its natural superiority to any, forms of "compulsory thrift."

Unless there a r e THE CENSUS OF unexpected difficulties HORSES, the census of horses which the War Office. is making ought very soon to provide such an adequate available reserve for mobilisa- tion as no country has hitherto enjoyed. Mr. Haldane estimates that in this country about 500,000 horses are well suited to military requirements, and of that number there is actual and immediate need for 156,000. At present the War Office has on register 43,000 animals, rather more than one-third being in its possession, and the rest being registered for an annual fee of ss. It will be necessary, therefore, to make record of 113,000 more. In preparation for this undertaking test censuses haveibeen made by the police of certain locali- ties, and, judging by them and by other investigations,there is good reason to believe the, supply should easily be assured.. The complete census will be entrusted to' the police, -who will be required to compile schedules of horses within the district under , authority which appear to. fulfil specified I requirements. No compulsion, it should be noted, is to be laid upon owners to suppry information. The returns will be submitted to military examination by Yeomanry officers, and selection will be made. until the arrangements are met. Schemes will then be formulated in elaborate detail under the

direction of the General Officer Com- manding, so as to make instant mobilisa- tion at all times practicable. Payment for animals taken over would be made after- wards. There are several questions upon this interesting scheme which require answer, and which will, no doubt, be answered in due course. If the register is to be of permanent value, it will be necessary to make a census almost annually. Horses may be sold, or they may lose their fitness; of course, they may die. How will those on the register be kept under constant supervision? A test mobilisation ought to be arranged so as to make the preparation for -war of real worth. Will owners be given to understand that they must submit to at least one false alarm, to prove their alacrity to surrender their serviceable animals to the State?

NOTES AND NOTIONS

Our readers' attention is drawn to the interesting article on page 8 detailing the progress and expectations of the Garden Suburbs Movement locally. We would just point out here that the article reassures those people who have expressed doubt as to the co-operators' intentions to provide work- men's houses on the estates at prices suited to the pockets of modest wage earners.

** * *

Congratulations to the Apollo Prize Male Choir and the Stockton Heath Choral Union on their brilliant performances on Monday. To capture £55 as well as some handsome trophies in a single day's competitions was an achievement of which they have full reason to be proud, and Mr. Harry Berrey, the talented conductor, is deserving of special praise for the way in which he led his choirs to victory.

** * *

General Sir lan Hamilton, who on Tuesday relinquished the Southern Command in order to become Adjutant-General of the Forces, recently set a fine example to his men by signing the pledge. He was inoved to this decision partly by the reflection that in his 37 years' service some 10,000 quarts of duti- able liquor had passed down his throat, and partly by the excellent Army tradition that officers should not say to their men "Go on," but "Come on." Sir lan's motto has always been "Trust the men," and few generals have been more deservedly popular.

a - We notice that the Health Committee has declined to entertain the proposal, submitted by Miss Broadbent, for the erection of a municipal lodging-house for women. In view of the recent report on housing conditions in Warrington, arid the drastic condemnation of many of the conditions obtaining in the common lodging-houses of the Borough,there seems to be plenty of justification fol. Miss Broadbent's proposal, and it should not be lightly put on one side. We hope the Council and the public will, on Tuesday, be favoured with some explanation of the Health Com- mittee's attitude on the question. *

It is very probable that the Aldermanic vacancy due to the death of Alderman Ellison will be filled on Tuesday by the elevation of Councillor James Evans, who is the senior Councillor, with eleven years' unbroken service. Mr. Evans has always been a pains- taking member of the Council, and in recent years he has given very close attention to the responsible and multifarious duties associated with his chairmanship of the Health Committee. He is not a man to make enemies, and we feel sure that his election will be a unanimous and popular one. * * *

The allowance or return of duty on motor- spirit used otherwise than for supplying motive-power for motor-cars, the rebate of half the duty to trade-motors and motor- buses, hackney carriages, &c., and the exemption from licence duty for such vehicles, were all announced in the Budget speech. The anly addition is the drawback on the export of motor-spirit which has already paid duty. " Motor-spirit "is defined as any inflammable hydrocarbon (including any mixture of hydrocarbons and any liquid containing hydrocarbon), capable of being used for reasonably efficient power in pro- pelling a motor-car, - •* * *

One of the most interesting "Life Stories" which we have yet been favoured with is that contributed this week by Mr. William Bennett of Padgate, the respected father of Alderman Bennett. He relates much about the early history of Padgate and the east end of Warrington which is quite fresh to the present generation and may be considered an additional chapter of local history. We are glad to note also that Mr. Bennett still exercises some paternal authority over his son the Alderman and very properly reproves him for his handwriting. We have often wondered in deciphering some of the Alder- man's M.S.S. whether he preferred and used a pin to a pen! * * The sale of the famous Holbein has raised the question of the effect of the Death Duties upon the retention in this country of historic works of art. Mr. Lloyd-George had already, in his Budget speech, fore- shadowed a concession which would exempt not only, as under the existing law, settled property which was of historic, national, or scientific interest from Death Duties, but also unsettled property, so long as it was not sold. He has now added a qualification to include" artistic" as well as" historical " or "national " interest in works of art. It can no longer be pleaded in excuse of the sale of great artistic treasures,as the Holbein "Duchess of Milan," that the Death Duties have forced the owner's hand.

Tha Whitsuntide - manceuvres of the Territorirls have shown (says the Man- chester Evening News " correspondent) the superiority of the town to the country-bred soldier. It used to be quite thereverse. But the yeomen who formerly provided the bulk of our country-bred army men has pretty nearly disappeared, and there are only left on the average a much inferior class of men! The 12s. per week labourer is nothing like so) good a soldier as the artizan in the town, who lives better and exercises his brains to a greater extent. Mr. John Burns, who is a keen observer, told the writer that he had prepared a list of the surrenders in the Boer war, and he. found that they were almost 'entirely, made up of. men brought up in the country, while the regiments which were most successful were those made up of town' bred men. The fact is that brains are more than ever required in the private soldier in actual. warfare. * *

Mr. W. A. Baillie-Grohman, wrotes to the "'Times ". on Wednesday to give us some "concrete details" about .the way property is taxed in Austria. He is executor ,for a relative who. died last year in that country. She derived her income from two ,houses (let in flats) in Vienna: The income she derived from' the houses amounted in the. year that comes under my , personal cognisance to £1,977: After paying the somewhat heavy city or municipal taxes, rates of various sorts, cost or adininistering the houses

and repairs, which were rathpr than usual, a ne income of £1,050 was left, and upon this I Was+ called upon to pay for the year an income-tax of £2l 25., or 2 per cent. on net receipts Thus, out of a total income of £1,977, £948 had to be paid in rates and taxes and repairs. Unearned incriment in this country still owes more than a little to the' State. By the way, is Austria likely to be one of the foreign countries where according to Mr. Balfour, British capital is shortly going to " fly " to ? * * *

Warringtonians will be keenly interested in to-day's brass band contest at New Brighton Tower, for the Perfection Soap Works Band will make another bold bid for the splendid trophy which they were fortu- nate enough to win two years ago but had to relinquish last year. The band was prob- ably never in better form and we look to them to add to their laurels and to the honour of the "good old town" in the musical world to-day.

An American newspaper says that Jack London, the novelist, on his last visit to New York, was introduced to a musician in a cafe. "I, too, am a musician in a small way," said London. "My musical talent was once the means of saving my life. There was a great flood in our town in my boyhood. When the water struck our house, my father got on a bed and floated with the stream until he was rescued." "And you—?" asked the musician. "Well," said London, "I accompanied him on the piano."

The statement was made at the Royal Institution the other day that cannibalism, on analysis of the "food," was found to be man's ideal diet. The phrase "dog eat dog" seems to lend colour to the theory that man eat man may after all be the proper thing. But on the face of it, it is not palatable whatever it may be on the tongue. Lest any of our readers should be alarmed at the pros- pect of so old a custom as cannibalism being grafted on to our modern civilization we may add that Mr. Eustace Miles refutes the medical dietary contention. Now Mr. Miles feeds on nuts and wins tennis championships thereby. If not manly his menu is at least nutritious! Choose ye each your dish! * *

Mr. Lloyd-George was the central figure on Tuesday of a series of pleasant and interesting functions on his native heath. Early in the day the heath had become (as might be expected) a golf course, which the Chancellor of the Exchequer opened. Mr. Lloyd-George was made a life-member, played a game of golf and made a speech at a luncheon. A debt, he declared, was due to Mr. Balfour and other statesmen for popularising golf," a game conducing to better, healthier, and saner life." The Chancellor added that "a man was all the better for the time which, within reason, he spent on the golf links." The last function was the most interesting of all, for at it Mr. Lloyd-George received an address from those who were his fellow- pupils at the National School at Llanystumdwy, the pretty little village a mile and a half out bf Cricieth. It is a far cry from theie to Downing-street, and happy is the nation where the gap can be bridged by natural ability, unbacked by the adventitious aids of birth or fortune.

Warringtonians for Canada. The Allan line steamer, Virginian, which left Liver- pool on Friday afternoon last week, had on board quite a numerous company of Warringtonians who are bound for the Dominion of Canada where they intend to make their future homes. The party included Miss Elsie Bond, eldest daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Henry Bond, Causeway-avenue, Warrington, who is going out to be. married to Mr. J. P. Hudson, of Montreal, formerly of Warrington, and now in the audit office bf the Grand Trunk Railway, of Canada ; Mr. and Mrs. Alfred Beckett (the lady being the recently married daughter of Mr. Robert Lowe, Latch- ford) who are going to Vancouver; and Mr. and Mrs. Summerfiekl and family, of Stockton Heath, who came over from Vancouver about two years ago and are now returning. There was quite a large party of relatives and friends of the travellers on the Liverpool Landing Stage, and they had a very hearty send-off on their long voyage. Warrington Cycling Club. Owing to the very unsettled state of the weather the tour arranged for the Whitsuntide holidays to Sannbach and Ellesmere was abandoned. Several parties of members however had runs to Knutsford, Little Leigh, and to other. places in the vicinity of Warrington on Saturday and Monday and the outings are reported to have been successful. The start of the Bowling Competition at Sankey was made on Wednesday evening, and there was a good attend- ance of bowlers. The first round of the Cup Com- petition was practically played off, which will enable the second round, and the first Consolation Competi- tion to be proceeded with .on Wednesday next. The turf was in excellent condition, and there was an exhibition of some really clever bowling. The Thursday evenings run to Bucklow Hill waa favoured with fine weather and good roads, and in consequence there was a good turnout of members. The return journey was made via Lymm. The club's arrangements for the coming week are :—To-day's run to Oulton Park; Tuesday, the Council meeting of the National Cyclists' Union (Liverpool centre) at headquarters, when the N.C.U. delegates will as well be entertained by the Club, (at that meeting the W.C.C. members are invited to be present)_; bowling at Sankey on Wednesday; the run to Halton on Thursday.

Nationil Children's Home —Street Collection.

A street collection has been arranged. to be made in Warrington on Saturday, June 12th, in aid of the National Children's Home and Orphanage, Bonner- road, London, N.E. which is one of the largest Child- saving Institutions in the world. It was iestablished in 1869 and has of Tecent years made very rapid pro, gress. .More than 400 children were received last year, and 2,000 are now in its care. The Home has branches in London, Birmingham, Lancashire, Yorkshire, Cheshire. Farnborough, (Hants), Alverstoke (Hants), Chipping Norton, Chadlington, Essex, Isle of Man and Canada.. A new Southern Branch is about to be established at Harpenden (Herts). A sanatorium for Childred threatened with Consumption is also about to be established. Every branch is conducted on the family system and special care is given to crippled and afflicted children. Children are received without regard to creed. The most needy child ,has always the first claim. The boyi and girls receive a thorough elementary educationand industrial training. Children are received from every part of the Kingdom, from the Colonies and abroad. Of the 8,009 children who have passed through the ;sTational 'Children's Home, over 2,000 hove been sent to Canada where they have done remarkably well. The others have for the most part remained in this country, and very many of them keep up a very friendly association with the Institution. Reports and full particulars of the work can be obtained on application to the Principal, Rev. Dr. Gregory, National Children's Home, Bonner-road, London, S.E. Mr. William Healey of 9, Worsley-street, Folly-lane, is the local hon. secretary for the street collection and he also will be pleased to give information as to the work, and receive offers of assistance, from friends willing to help. .

THE ROYAL VISIT. Latest Arrangements.

The various sub-committees appointed to make the arrangements for the visit of their Majesties the King and Queen to Warrington on Tuesday, July 6th, have lost no time in getting to work and the preparations are nosy well in hand. _ _

The committee which has the charge of the decorations met at the Town Hall yesterday morning and decided to make an inspection of the route on Tuesday, next for the purpose of determining the positions for the erection of arches and the decoration of the streets. It Is proposed, to erect a triumphal arch near Bruche Bridge and another at the other boundary of the borough at Sankey Bridges. The sub-committee appointed to arrange for the provision of stands and the allocation of seats has decided to erect stands in Liverpool-road (near St. Luke's Church), St. Elphin's Park, Manchester-roal, and in Howard Place, for the 'accommodation of school children between the ages of four and thirteen. Stands will also be erected on each Side of the steps in front of the Town Hall, for the. members of the Council, magistrates, and those holding official positions in the borough. It is possible that there will be additional stands at Market Gate and in front of the Education Offices and Garven House. PREPARING THE ROADS.

At the monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council on Wednesday, Mr. W. Houghton said their Majesties the King and Queen would pass along the road in the Council's district on their yisit to Warrington on July 6th. He suggested that instructions should be given to the Surveyor to see that the roads were well watered, if there happened to be dusty weather on that occasion. The Surveyor said that since the last meetingle had met Lord Derby and Mr. Schofield (County 'Surveyor) in the matter. Mr. Schofield had brought ,the question of tar-spraying before the ChairmEn of the Main Roads Committee who had authorized him to tar-spray the whole of the Liverpool-road from Raiahill to Warrington and it was expected that the Manchester-road would be treated in the same way. It was not suggested that any of the cost of preparing the roads should fall on the District Council.

The Council granted Mr. Newcombe authority to comply with any directions of the County Surveyor on the understanding that the Council would kiot be asked to bear any of the cost.

THE TERRITORIAL REVIEW.

An importint function. in which great interest is being taken throughout West Lancashire, is the Royal review of the Territorial Forces at Knowsley on July 7th: The local expenses of the review there, together with the corresponding view of the East Lancashire " Terriers " at Worsley on the following day, will cost approximately £lO,OOO. Responding to the appeal of the Lord-Lieutenant and others, many gentlemen from all parts of the county have already joined the guarantee fund, but it is desired to spread the liability over a far wider number, and to have the double event • treated as one of county concern. The burden will, of course, be lightened by the sale of tickets for the stands, and the applications, at any rate for the seats .at Knowsley, are encouraging.

Tariff Reformers and Joinery. Sir,—Last week I referred to the unfortunate choice , Tariff Reformers make of trades to illustrate their contention that we are being ruined by mg free imports. The boot trade was a case in point. A secord trade that has done service on Tariff Reform platforms is that of carpenters and joiners. This trade is specially interested in house frames, doors, and like fittings. The figures given below indicate the steady decrease of these imported goods. I give alongside the per centages of unemployment in this trade. Suppose that the imports of these goods had a. direct relation to unemployment 'we should have expected that the greater the imports the greater the unemployment, instead of this the exact reverse is the fact, the figures speak for themselves.. Whatever be the complicated causes which result in so large a proportion of unemployment it is certainly not free imports.
