Tariff Reorm and Industrial Strife.

Sir,—ln the otherwise excellent report of my speech last week at the Liberal Club which you were good enough to give in last Saturday's Examiner," there is one slight error. I did not speak of the danger of International strife that would arise if we went in for Protection, although the Tariff Reformer's policy, if it, were ever tried, would certainly not be calculated to improve the relations between the British Empire and foreign countries. What I referred to in my speech. however, was the danger of Industrial strife at home, which would arise, if by the adoption of the Protective system we raised the cost of British manufactures all round, and therefore struck a blow at the commercial supremacy which British manufacturers and mer- chants enjoy in the great neutral markets of the world. It is certain that as our export trade seriously declined manufacturers would insist on a reduction of wages in order to bring down their cost of manufacture, and would tell their workpeople that in that way alone could they keep up output and find them employment. It is equally certain that with the cost of living increased all round the employes would very.naturally reply that far from any reduction in wages what they required was an advance. This country finished with Protection many a long year since and is not going to be ensnared into it again by our friends the Tariff Reformers, however artfully they may spread their nets. But if we did commit such a suicidal, such an utterly stupid act of folly, far from increasing employment we should, by damaging the greatest industries in the country, diminish it, and, as I venture ,to submit, at the same time trarel along the road that leads to industrial strife. The bitter feeling aroused where, under the shelter of a Protective tariff, a few individuals and a few monopolies would be enabled to enrich themselves at the expense of the whole community—that sense of injustice would also tend in the same direction towards social unrest and industrial warfare. .

As to the debasing and demoralising effect on political life in the country, it, is enough to watch what has been going on recently in America. A good deal has been said and written about this lately, but. I have not come across anything which sums up the present position of things in the United States more graphically, forcibly and humorously than two extracts from the American " World," which are published in an article in last Saturday's issue of the "Economist." The article is only a short one, and if you are able to find space for it, I -am sure the readers of the " Examiner" who are following this great fiscal con- troversy will find the facts there summarised well worth noting.—Yours etc.,

A. 11. CROSFIELD.

lloylake, June 2nd, 1909.

We append the article frotn •The Economist referred to by Mr. Crosfield ;

THE MORAL AND POLITICAL SEQUEL OF TARIFF REFORM. .

Nobody can possibly appreciate the full con- sequences of Tariff Reform until he has studied it in its most perfect ani complete shape in the United States of America. By way of illustration, a correspondent sends us two short leading articles wh ch have recently appaared in "The World" of New York, and we venture to reproduce them in the hope thatlonest tariff reformers in this country will reconsider their opinions :

• A REAL INFANT INDUSTRY. "Benjamin Tillman is the only true-blue Protec- tionist in the Senate. Nobody else this session has presented the case of a real infant industry. The South. Carolina tea-grower who raises 15,000 pounds a year has to compete with thepauper labour of China and Ceylon, which ship their product free into the United States. i The competition s excessively severe, and Americans are reduced to the hateful necessity of drinking cheap, foreign-grown tea. If Sanator Aldrich would kindly let Senator Tillman's friend write his own tea schedule into the Tariff Bill, as he bas some of his own New England friends, the South Cirolina tea trust would be able to charge any price that it liked. It might soon declare dividends that would make the Standard Oil's look insignificant.

Here are 85,006,000 of people—a magnificent home market—and only one infant tea industry. Hera are millions of acres of fertile land capable of cultivation, millions of American men ready to cultivate them, and only one tea plantation in the whole country. Cheap tea, like cheap clothing, every Protectionist knows to be a curse. Shut out foreign tea by a prohibitory duty and not only will the South Carolina tea industry enjoy a boom, but every paternal and maternal American will feel a just pride in having nursed another infant industry. Make the duty high enough, and some day Senator TilNan's South Carolina friend may ship tea to China and undersell *the Chinese at home."

Here is another criticism of the kind of thing that is done and said in a great democracy which has carried out the programme of our Tariff Reform League. We again reprint verbatim from "The World."

===== 0002601_19090605_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WRETCHED VICTIMS.

"So it is not the tariff after all that raised prices to the consumer. That is a " natent economic fallacy of the Democrats." Senator Hale says so, and he knows, for he has been a high-tariff man all his life. When Senator Hale votes for a tariff duty on print paper, in the manufacture of which his son is interested ; when Senator Scott votes for protection for glassware from his own factory; when Senator Elkins votes for pro- tection for coal from his own mines; when Senator Guggenheim votes for protection for lead produced by the Guggenheim smelters ; when Representative Fordney votes for protection for lumber from his own mills, they are not seeking an increase in prices for the benefit of themselves and their relatives and partners. The tariff does not affect prices. This is a stupid delusion on the part of the consumer. If he doubts it he has Senator Hale's solemn word for it that " the householder is paying nothing to a protective tariff that builds up American industry." It is the retailer who is the guilty wretch, the son of iniquity, who makes high prices and practices extortion upon the consumer.

The foreigner, it seems, is no longer paying the tariff. He used to, bat that was before ths Protection- ists changed their arguments. The cansurner does not pay it, a 3 Senator Hale has proved. There are no con- sumers anyhow, for Senator Lodge says they are all orodiicer4. Naturally the retailer does not pay it, for he robs the consumer, who is a producer. Who does pay the tariff? N‘hy, it must be the tariff beneficiary himself, like Senator Hales son and Senator Scott and Senator Elkins and Senator Guggenheim. He is the wretched victim of the beneficent system, and out of charity to himself he wants duties raised so as to be relieved of his burdens." Now, what we should like to ask our readers is whether they desire that these controversies should be imported into, our political life. Here, as in every other country, whatever its form of government, there is, and always will be, a certain amount of jobbery in connection with Government contracts and Govern- ment patronage. But these are minor evils always kept in check by publicity: The making of a tariff with the avowed intention of helping special producers at the expense of the general consumer is a very different matter. Instead of an occasional shower of improper patronage we shall be plunged into an ocean of corruption. Honest men will avoid public life as they would avoid the pillory. Dishonest men will seek election to the House of Commons as a means of making a fortune. There will be no necessity for direct payment of members. There will be no diffi- culty about replenishing party chests.

BRUTAL ASSAULT ON A CONSTABLE. SMART SENTENCES. The story of a brutal assault epon a police officer was heard at the Borough Police Court on Wednesday. when Richard Clanay, Catherine Clancy and Joseph Clench of Pierpoint-street. were ch raged with asselting P.C. Thomas Henry Faulkner while acting in the execution of his duty. Richard and Catherine Clancy pleaded guilty but Joseph Clancy "not gailtv." The Chief Constable stated that on Saturday night the 2nd of last month, Faulkner was on duty in the Bewsey district. He heard a disturbance taking place at the rear of Pierpoint-Streat and Catherine-street. He went down a passage and saw a number of men and women sitting (loan and drinking. As it was very dark Faulkner turned his light on. The prisoner Richard Clancy immediately jumped up and shouted "Don't shine your lamp on me," and struck the officer a violent blow in the eye. Faulkner attempted to take him into custody when Joseph Clancy csme out of 15, Pierpoint - street, grabbed the officer by the throat and endeavoured to release his brother. Meanwhile the female prisoner went, into the house and began throwing cups and basins at the officer. She was afterwards seen groping round the wall and picked up a black glass bottle with which she struck Faulkner a violent blow on the back of the head, knocking him senseless. A little girl named Mabel :\inrsh rendered some valuable assistance. While the pfficer was on the ground she picked up his cape and pluckily held it before his face, thus warding off the hies's. Otherwise the prisoners might have been there on a more serious charge. Chief Inspector Williamson and Sergeant Mercalf were in the vicinity and heard a' police whistle blow. They at. once proceeded there and made infinities with the result that the three prisoners were arrested. They (the prisoners) 'might say that they ware under the influence of drink, but it would be moved that that was not the case. The officers found Faulkner in a house close by. having his wounds bathed. He was afterwards taken to the Infirmary and attended by Dr. McCune. He tuts still an out-patient of the institteion. P.C. Faulkner stated that on the night in question, he heard a disturbance taking place at the rear of Pier- point-street. He proceeded there and saw a number of men and women drinking. Richard Clancy went up to him and asked him what he wanted there and why be was shining his light upon him. Witness told him that it was his own business. Richard Clancy then struck itness a blow on the left temple. Witness struggled with him and while doing so, Joseph caught him by the throat. At the same time, the female prisoner struck him two blows behind the ear with a bottle. Witness relaxed his hold of Richard Clancy who had his legs intertwined with witness's, in order to thrown him to the ground. The female 'prisoner then struck him a third blow with the bottle behind the ear and knocked him insensible. Mabel Marsh, aged 15 years, of Pierpoint-street described the assault upon the police officer, and said that she held the cape in front of the officer's face in order to shield him from the blows. Chief Inspector Williamson said he was in Horse- market-street on the night in question ssith Sergeant Meacock and a number of other officers when he heard a police whistle blown in Bewsey-street. From what they vre,e told they mode all haste to Pierpoint-street and there friend Faulkner in a house having his head bathed. Witness and Sergeant Meacock made full inquiries in the dist•ict and arrested Richard Clancy at 116, Piernoint-street. They found Catherine Clancy in a house in Catherine-street. Afters, parleyine for a quarter of an hour they succeeded in getting her down to the police station. She was not drunk but acting like a lunatic. They also founds quantity of broken crockery and the bottle which was used in assaulting the constable. The chief constable said be thought it was a beer bottle. The female prisoner (interrupting) "It. was a dandelion stout bottle." Dr. McCnne, of the Warrinst on Infirmary, stated that wben Faulkner was admitted he was in a collapsed condition. He was suffering from a wound about ii inches long on the left side of the head. It was situated over the blood vessels and that accounted for the loss of blood. Special mcasure to stop the flow of blood had to he resorted to. In consequence of his collepssd condition witness detained Faulkner for a week iu_the hospital. The Chief Constable stated that nll three prisoners had very had charaelers. Richard Clnncey had been before the Court 12 times preciously, Catherine Clancy six times, and Joseph Clancy 15 times. They were the worst characters in Warrington and the only redeeming feature was that they could work for their living. With regard to Mabel Marsh's evidence Joseph C7ancy stated that she had said more than she knew. The Chairman (Mr. j. W.Ha,•ward) stated that the magistrates had given the case every consideration. Richard Clancy must go to gaol' for 6 months with hard labour, Catherine Clancy for A months with hard labour and Joseph for 3 months with hard labour. On being removed below the female prisoner shouted " I II have my own hack when I come out." WARRINGTON GARDEN SUBURBS.

How th 2 Schema is PiArassing.

In view of the recent Conference on the Housing Question and the striking andsuggestive report which appeared in our issue of Saturday last, it may be interesting to give some infot mation as to the present position of the local Garden Suburbs movement, which is specially recommended by the National Hoasino• Reform Council as one of the best solutions of the existing difficulty. , It will be remembered that the Warrington Garden Suburbs Company have acquired two estates, one at Great Sankey, with an acre of about 20 acres and one at Grappenhall with an area of about 22 acres; that plans have been prepared for laying these out in a very attractive manner; with ample recreation grounds and open spaces; that it is a fundamental condition that every house shall have a garden; that not more than an average of twelve houses are to be built to the acre ; and that any dividends the shareholders of the comwnymay receive are strictly limited to five per cent. It will also be rememiered that a Co-operative Workinginen's Society called the," Warrington Tenants, Limited," has been registered under the Industrial and Provident Societies Acts; and that the foundation stones of their first four houses at Great Saekay were laid by Lady G'reenall and Mr. Ebenezer Howard, the founder of the first Garden City, on the Ist October last, amid every evidence of public interest and enthusiasm. The designs for the houses were prepared by Messrs. J. E. Wright, Garnett and Wright; the work has been admirably executed by Mr. F. W. Wright, builder, Latchford; and the houses are now ready for occupation. They are very pleasantly situated, looking out across the estuary of the Mersey to Norton priory and Halton Castle, and the style of the architecture, with the handsome chimneys,overhan 'cring eaves and red, tiled roofs, is most attractive. The internal arrangements are exceedingly convenient, and each house contains two entertaining rooms, kitchen, scullery,three bedrooms,bathroona.&c.,with all thelatest modern accesiories, and has, of course, a fair-sized garden. Theirents have been fixed at twelve shillings pet week, free of rates, for the two end houses, and us. (id. per week for the middle ones. It has been suggested that these rents are beyond the reach of the ordinaty working-man, and this fact has led to some misconception ; but if the necessary financial support is forthcoming, it is the intention of the Tenants' Society to erect additional houses at a much lower rental as early as possible, and, indeed, to ulti- mately cater for all classes. Four other houses are to be built forthwith by private entexprize, two at Grappenhall and two at Great Sankey, and the parent company are prepared to lease suitable plots on either estate to builders or to individual investors at very moderate ground rents. It is a little surprising that the working-classes, and especially the local believers in co operation, have not supported the Tenants' Society better, as it is estab- lished on a purely co-operative basis, and has many advantages to offer. Each tenant member is required to eventually invest the sum of :t5O in the Society, but this can be done by small instalments, and the sum invested is expectedlto produce a return of five per cent., to which the actual dividends are limited, all profits over and above this amount berng allocated to the tenant members as Share Capital or applied in the reduction of their rents. From the Report of the Housing Connell, it is evident that n large proportion of the skilled workmen of Warrington receive wages which average from 30s. to £2 per week. Many of these have money invested in other ways at a much lower rate than five per 'cent. ; most of them can pay , a reasonable rental; and the majority of them are at present living in houses which are ugly, inconvenient and densely crowded, time number of houses, in many instances, according to Mr. H. R. Aldridge's report, amounting to as much as forty to the acre. By joining the Tenants' Society, they would be able to get a better return for their money than many of them get at present ; to live in healthy and pleasant surroundings ; to have the management of the own property; to know that their rents could never be excessive; . and to themselves receive whatever profits were made by the Society beyond the dividend of five per cent In evety other place except Warrington in which the Co-operative Societies have been established, they appear to 'have been a,great success. There are now something like a dozen of them in existence, and most of them are progressing by leaps and bounds. The Ealing Society has erected property to the value of close upon £lOO,OOO, the Garden City tenants £79,000, the Hampstead tenants £67,000, the Harborne tenants £47,000, the Manchester tenants :£26,000, and the Oldham tenants a similar amount. By the end of the present year, the total value of the buildings belting- ing to these societies is expected to amount to fully half a Million pounds. We believe that practically all of them pay a dividend after the first year, and it is a little surprising that, whilst so many other places realize the advantages of such a scheme, the working- men of Warrington should hesitate and lag so much behind. The success of the local experiment, which was started expressly for their benefit, will vety largely depend upon the support which they are them- selves prepared to give to it. "Heaven helps those who help themselves," caul unless the local public (and especially the working-classes) are satisfied with the conditions disclosed in the report which was sub- mitted last week, by the National Housing Reform Council, they will surely do their very utmost to assist the promoters in their public spirited proposals The Secretary of the Tenants' Society is Mr. George H. Smith, 15, Lovely-lane, and those who would like to join the Society are requested to communicate with him. We may add that the general public are cordially invited to visit the Company's estates, includ- ing the four houses which have just been completed, and that the fullest information will be given to all inquirers on application to the Secretary of the parent company, Mr. Arthur Bennett, Chartered Accountant, Market Gate Chambers, Warrington.

There was a fair attendance at the meeting of the Society on Sunday last. Mr Joseph Owen presided and spoke upon "The Apostle Paul." Mr. Walter Twist gave an address on " Tne Conversion of Ssul," in which he referred to Saul's ideal, learning and ability as a man, and to his farly opposition to the Chr.stian Church. But a point was r.-ached when Christ spoke to him, closed his eyes to what he previously thought was right and opened then to a new and living way. Bewsey-road Wesley Guild

Bewsey-road Wesley Guild has always something to suggest for a Bank Holiday, and the proposal that they should visit the National Children's Home and Orphan- age, at Frodsham on Monday hist met with ft very ready response. About 80 guilders set off on the train and also a few on bicycles for the hilly village. At the tinge of their departure the weather was rather inclement: but the cyclists toiled on, for was not that a holiday? aad also a phrase "Toil on, faint not;to-morrow comes the song " rang in their ears and their expectations were realised when within a hour later Old Sol shone out, in all his glory. Arrived there the party wended their way to the Orphanage, where 150 Lanett- shire and Cheshire children are accommodated and were conducted over the home by Mr. S. Walton, the governor. The children were happy and con- tented and had spirit enough to indulge in games w ith the guilders and very food ti,ve was obtained thrnigh the co-operation of the parties. Tea wns provided for the visitors by the Orphanage at a moderate cluirge and after that they were at liberty to follow their own inclinations, but the vast majority stayed and p'ayed with the children. .Some of the ladies were kind enough to give the children rides on their bicycles, and when once on they did at all like the idea of getting og, became in many cases it was the first time they had ever mounted a cycle. The Guilders returned home at 7.30, satisfied that a very enjoyable afternoon had ban spent.

Bold-street Wesleyan Church

The Rev. Thomas Miller preached on Sunday evening in the above church to a good congregation. He took his text from John xvi., 8-11, and in the course of his sermon said that the Spirit did not dethrone intellect nor was He d-feated by advancing civilization. Educa- tion would not r‘inder his work nugatory. There was little doubt that our improved education was destruc- tive to flagrant sin. Drunkenness and lust, still open enough to disgust thoughtful mind. were not as flatant as formerly. Our streets were sfill frequented by the worthless and vicious, but there had been is great improvement. Common scenes of half a century ago would never he tolerated to-day. Vice ltirkei in s wet; n•iw then it was open. The standard of civilization was higher to-day than ever. There was still room for sorrow, but there was great cause for thankfufulness. As science, esneciall physiology, was more widely understood,.and education was perfected, public ',pinion would make impossible many of our national vices. We were now in the transition stage. Some Curistian preachers were very anxious because the extreme forms of penetence which were associated with revivals of religion were absent to-day. It required less of the spirit's agency to con- vict the miners of Kingwood and Cornwall of sin, than it required to change the moral conception of men who were educatedand highly moral in their judgments and character. In the case of the miners, reason was on the side of the preacher. Few arguments %sere necessary to prove that a wife beater, or a harlot was a sinner ; it required greas force to c.invince a man who was upright and true, but who found difficulty in religions matters, that his life was 'as complete a failure as the miners. Concliding be said that amidst their discouragements, let them remember that they were not alone, for the Spirit of God was working with them.

Holy Trinity Church. 81-centenary Sermon by the Bishop.

It is now 200 yelra since Holy Trinity Church was built, and to celebrate such a notable anniversary the first of a series of special services do%ering a period of three Sundays, was held last Sunday morning, when the Lnrd Bishop of Liver- pool preached. The Alasor (Alderman Forshaw) paid an official visit to the Church accompanied by the members of the Corporation, magistrates and other townsmen.

The procession uhich left the Town flail at 10. i 5 :4its headed by the Blue Coat School hind, his Worship had his chaplin (the Bev. 0. C. Brigg.) and the Deputy Mayor (Alderman Solt-thrust) on his left, and on his right the Deputy Town Clerk (Mr. Hallway), and included the Fire Brig +de, St. John Ambulance Corps. and a detachment of police. The members of the Cor- poral ion present wete Aldermen Bennett, Burton and 'Minion, and Councillors Ashcroft, Barton, Birtles, Broadhurst, Craik, Halliwell, Evans, Folton-Smith, Locker, Roger ma Wilson ; officials, Mr. T. H. Spieks (assistant town clerk),Mr. T.Longdin (oorough surveyor), the Chief Constable (Mr. M. Nicholls), Mr. J. Moore Murray (director of education), Mr. W. S. Haddock (gas engineer), Mr. W. S. Howard (assistant secretary and ashier of the gas department), and Mr. W. T. Flood (inspector of nuisances) ; local magistrttes, Messrs. R. T. Fairclough, J. W. Hayward and C. S. Burgess. Amongst others pres-nt were : lfe•mrs Houghton, T. D. Amos, LI, Ferguson, T. Mason, H. Isherwood and E. Rose.

There was it large coneregation at the service, which was conducted by the Vicar (the Rev. E. C. E.Cerleton) and the Mayor's chaplain. The hymns snug were "o God, our help in ages past." "Come, Holy spirit "and Lord enthroned in heavenly splendour." The Bis Hoe, in the course of an eloquent sermon, said they bad come there that day to celebrate the hi-centenary of that Church, they had met to thank Ged IN, for all the blessings to which that Church had been in blessing might be equally fruitful and equally blessed to our children and to our children's tthhaetciv oiocirs life of errington for 200 years and to pray children. That day *their thoughts went back for two centuries and they conjured up the picture of England as it then was. Queen Anne was on the throne, the Archbishop of York, Archbishop Sharp waft her devoted and wise counsellor; the Church of England had reached almost its zenith, its clergy were remark- able for their learning—there Inas a current saying that the Anglican clergy were "the wonder of the world." The churches were well filled ard as an instance of the life and activity of the church they saw the foundation of the Society for Promoting Christian Knowledge that sought to leaven our countrymen with the knowledge of God, and the Society for the Propagation of the G meet which sought to send the Gospel to our colonies abroad. John IVesley, when that charch was first born, was a little boy growieg up under the strict hut fruitful discipline of his father and mother at. Epworth Rectory. At home the un:on between England and Scotland had just been completed with honour to both countries. In Ireland those harsh and penal lass, which could never be justified were sowing those seeds of hatred and estrangement,of which they that day 200 years Pfter,e ere reaping the harvest. Abroad the great victories of that mili,ary genius, the Duke of Marlborough, had finally broken the French military supremacy on the continent which had lasted for more than 60 years, and on the sea the ravy of England was supreme. Coming nearer borne, Liverpool was a small and not very important town—just beginning to realise what it might be. It's Church of St. Peter, now the Cathedral, had been built some eight or nine years before, and at that time when the consecration servicts were held in that Church, the Liverpool Blue Coat School was being built and the first floating dock to which Liverpool owed its greatness was being made. Warrington was then a small pleasant country town on the banks of the Mersey, but it had changed, and now it occupied no mean position in South Lancashire. Changes had taken place during the 200 years that had passed. Referring to changes politically, his Lordship said the Reform Bill and similar measures had shifted the balance of power from the middle classes to the masses, and it rested with them to see that. the masses were well and religiously educated and leavened with the spirit of our Lord Jesus Christ, if the national spirit were to be all that it had been and the national greatness to remain intact. The period of the Church of Englsrid's glory in the re'gn of Queen Anne was followed by a period of degradation PM lifelessness. Thi-n John Wesley, George Whitfield and their comrades were used by God to bring about that guided awakening which they called the evangelical movement of the 18th century, and to plac9 in its proper rosition the great and lest, doctrine of the Atonement. In the 19th century came the great Oxford movement, which placed in its true position the greatand infinite doctrine of the Incarnation. To-day we were face to face with what might prove another great religious movement ,the object of which was to bring religion more and more into our daily life and to make it leaven arid permeate our business, politics, and pleasure, and not to be a thing apart. Continuing, the preacher said that socially there could be no doubt that the condition of our people had wonderfully changed and wonderfully improved. To-day in spite of so great problems—and they were great—both to Church and State it was a joy to know that our people as a whole were better housed, better fed, better clothed, better educated and far more intelligent than they were when the congregation gathered for the first time within the walls of that Church to consecrate it to God's service for ever. Two hundred years bad passed and still the same prayers,were read within those walls, and still the samegreat truths were preached. The fashion of the service might be somewhat different, and the great truths might be some- what altered, but the worship was still the same. Con.

tinning, he said that we on this hi-centehar y can give our best thanks to Almighty God for His. mercies to us as a country and as a Church during the past 200 years, by dealing fairly, kindly, and lovingly with those who are not members of our own branch of Christ's Holy Catholic Church ; but still love, perhaps better than we do, the same Lord—Jesus Christ." His Lordship appealed to his congreg 'lion to pay attention to prayer. Not until they prayed as their fore- fathers prayed could the Holy Spirit work in thera and through them. On th it the 20Uth auniversary of the Church, let them thank Go 1 for the past and humbly beseech Him to use it t bless Warringtou, as it had been blebt in the days that were gone. in the afternoon, the Vicar addressei a scholar's service and in the evening the Rev. James Carson, rector of Newton, preached a suruou in which he traced the history of the Church. The collections which were in aid of the Curacy Fund, amounted to about £l6.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.-

"Love, the Conqueror."

Mr. Frank Lindo is to be congratulated upon his new romantic play "Love, the Conqueror" which is ,conceived in a picturesquesetting of old English life and Offers plenty of thrilling adventure without being over-sensational. It deals with those momentous days when England was about to transfer her allegi- ance from James 11. to William of Orange and the author has succeeded in weaving a .story quite enthralling in its interest. The hero is Brian Norval, an outlaw and highwayman with an unenviable record who, however, proves capably of responding to the highest emotions and of hazarding his life from a sense of duty. Mr. Lindo himself takes this part in a dashing and powerful style and is a great favourite with the .audience. Miss Marion Wakeford is a charming \Mistress Beatrice Mortimer and infuses nnich-feeling into her interpret ition of a trying part. A really notable character study is presented by Mr. Harry Belford who, as Andrew Allbertree, impersonates the old-fashioned landlord of "The Blue Posts " to the life. Mr. Lawrence Doyle is convincing as Count Leon Lascelles whose unscrupulousness calls forth protests from the gallery, and Mr. Norton Shields plays the part of Sir ;John Mortimer very naturally. Mr. Charles Olive is a dignified William of Orange and Mr. W. Gregory as Nicholas entertains with some clever comic relief. The scenic effects are on a lavish scale and the incidental music is capably played by the orchestra under Mr. Northam Latham's conductorship.

THURSDAY'S MATINEE. MISS MABEL LOVE AS "LADY FREDERICK." The generous support accorded the experiment of a matinee performance of "Lady Frederick" by the Hutchison No. 1 Company on Thursday must have been at once both gratifying to the proprietary and an encouraging sign that the Warrington public is ready to respond to a i.y well-considered appeal for patronage. Miss Mabel Love as" Lady Frederick" in the comedy of that name which has already become a great London success was, of course, the greqt attraction. But the piece is by no means a one-character play, interest in its unfolding and working-out being divided between this and the equally strong character of Mr. Fouldes impersonated with just the right aegree of nonchalant insouciance by Mr. Robert Minster. To many to whom Miss Mabel Love's person- ality ir; known only through the medium of the übiquitous picture postcard, first acquaintance with the well-known actress would doubtless seem disappointing, for the part of Lady Frederick calls for a speaking voice and a pronounced manlier which one would scarcely associate with Miss Love's 'photo- graphic presentment. The assumption has however the merit of rapidly growing upon one. Without Attempting to outline the story—full as it is of pungently epigrammatic dialogue and equally strong situations—there need be no hesitation in saying that Miss Love's brogue and the quick resourcefulness of her actions throughout the play seemed a most natural thing, a feeling which so rapidly took possession of one that admiration of her acting quickly ripened into enthusiasm. Although interest centres chiefly in the intercourse of Lady Frederick and Mr. Fouldes there are several other characters which are both well-differentiated and well-worked into the warp and woof of the play's action and incident. Of these the Lady Mereston of Miss Celia Gordon is the most noteworthy, while the Captain Montgomery of Mr. Edward Bonfield and the Admiral Carlisle of Captain Gerald Gambier may be cited as excellent impersonations.

At the close of the comedy, which ran its length briskly and with great smoothness, there were several recalls of the two principal characters and many demonstrations of the audience's appreciation and good,A ill. It should be mentioned that the theatre orchestra— now after many years of dismal incompetency placed upon a proper footing in point of variety of instruments and executive ability—played a special programme of music between the acts under the direction of Mr. Norman Latham, in ,so capable a manner that it was a pleasure to listen to it.

RAT DESTRUCTION.

The Lister Institute of Preventive Medicine, report- ing upon the Danysz Virus Bacillus, has declared it entirely different from the bacillus which occasions typhoid fever, and adds that the extensive employment of the virus in many places without ill result to man- kind shows that the danger of human infection is small. In this connection it msy be mentioned that rats and mice are alwas liable to carry infection, and should therefore at all times be prevented from having access to food intended for human consumption.

The heat of the sun beating on the window panes at the house of Mr. Brewer, of Laughton Common, York- shire, set fire to the blinds, but fortunately an occupant of the house wds at hand, and the fire was extinguished before serious damage had been done.

OLD ENGLISH SPORTS

Successful Charitable Effort at Warrington

Although the weather was inclement, and therefore unfavourable to a certain degree for sports, the old English sports and gala, organised by the Warrington Charities Committee, in aid of the Infirmary and Nursing Association, were a grand success. The crowd. of 4,000 people, were at times subject to the temper of the clouds, but they held bravely on, for did they not think of the fine sport and fun to be witnessed right through the four hours of the gala ? The programme was an admirable one, and the interest of the spectators centred around each item. A variation from the usual routine of sports as provided by some of the children of Wycliffe and SI Ann's Schools% who took part in a Maypole dance. This was indeed fine, and received the applause it deservedly merited. Mr. H. Hope was a most energetic secretary, and to him no small praise must be given for the success of the event.

Mr. GEORGE WALKER said he wanted to express the feeling of the c9mmitt CP. and that was that they were greatly pleased that there had been such a large muster on such an unfavourable day. It would warrant the committee making the sports an annual institution for Whit-Monday. They thought that what they Ind seen that day would provide encourieement and good advertisement for another time. He had to apologise for the absence of the Mayoress. They had a lady in the ex-Mayoress (Mrs. Smlthurst) who was always ready to assist in every good work. and .he would now call upon her to distribute the prize. Mrs. SMETHURST then presented the prizes to the proud winners, after which a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to her, on the proposition of Mr. George Walker.

Alderman SMETHURST, renlying on behalf of his wife, said he was extremely obliged to them for the vote of thinks to his dear wife. He was sure that it gave her very g' eat pleasure to be there that afternoon and to see that they were engaged in such a glorious work in the raising of funds for our Warrington Infirmary. It was the first time they had had these sports and be hoped that it would not be the last; for it would bring our Infirmary much more before the public than it had been in the past. When he and his wife left office they promiaed the Mayor and Mayoress that they would do their best to assist them, and they had done so that afternoon by coming to take part in thost, proceedings in theunavoidable absence of the Mayor and Mayoress.

CHEAP AND QUICK THATCHING.

have often wondered why the Devonshire system of thatching has not been more eenerally adopted. I believe they call it" reed thatching," but am not qnite sure of the proper locil term. The straw is prepared in the winter in a barn or other building. It is thoroughly straightened and formed inth long sheets with the use of cheap string, and rolled up ready for the thatcher. When the ricks are put no the Tolls have only to be carried to the roof, spread and secured with wooden pegs, and so quickly can this be done that the corn,can be made safe as fast as it is harvested. If this plan were followed, the work of preparation in winter wo.uld be exactly suited to women, and their wage 3 would be well repaid to the farmer.—A. T. Matthews, in"Jountry Life."

THE NEW BYE-LAWS. The CLERK snbmitted the now bye-laws of the Council with regard to new streets and buildings. Mr. FURNESS called attention to a bye-law which provided that no ces7pool should be placed within ti distance of 50 feet from any building, and Faid he ;thought it would be impossible to carry it out strictly because the majority of the houses were so built that the land available would not admit, of the cesspool being placed such a distance from the building. Unles tb.-> Council was prepared to amend the bye-law the Sur- veyor would find it exceedingly difficult to carry it out. Mr. A. BrtooKs said 'cesspools were only necessary nowadays in case of isolated houses. If a builder wanted to erect small houses and could not make arrang.ments for the disposil of the sewage he would get into trouble. It was not a fit thing to have a cesspoot near a house.

The CLERK said it was better for the Council to have the power and if it became a matter for consideration in exceptional c tses, they could exercise their discretion.

The bye-laws were accepted and will he formally submitted for approval after the usual advertisement.

THE HORSE THAT WON THE DERBY.

MINORU (H. JONES UP).

Folks are still speaking about the King's Derby—how Minoru just got in front of the French horse at the post, the enthusiasm of the crowd when His Majesty left the Royal box to lead in the winner, and the singing of the National Anthem by a quarter of a million of people. Truly, the King's Derby of 1939 will be ever-memorable, and Minoru will be spoken about by generations to come. Our illustration shows the winning horse, with H. Jones up.

THE CONQUEST OF THE

-AIR. COUNT ZEPPELIN.

After ten years of hard work, during which he has spent two fortunes, Count Zeppelin has at last achieved a distinct measure of success with his dirigible Zeppelin 11. Leaving its base on Lake Constance on Saturday night, it covered 600 miles in thirty-seven hours without a stop—a performance that places all other airship records n the shade.

THE GOLF CHAMPION.

MR. R. MAXWELL.

Scotland is carrying everything before her ,in the golfing world. Miss Dorothy Campbell took the ladies' championship to Scotland, the Scottish international side soundly Whacked England, and now the amateur championship has been won by Mr. R. Maxwell, a Scotsman. And not only that, there were seven Scots- men in the last eight!

The respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were: Bankruptcies, 96 ; Deeds of Arrange- ment, 92—Total 188; being an increase of 28. The total number of Commercial Failures recorded during the 22 weeks of the present year is 3,470; the total number recorded in the corresponding 22 weeks.of last year was 10,677, allowing a decrease of 207.

WARRINGTON CHAPTERS. >

A STORY OF PETER LEGH

THE DONOR OF TRINL Y CHURCH.

The celebration of the Bi-Centenary of the Church of Holy Trinity recorded in this issue will make appropriate a reference to the generous landowner who gave the site and building two hundred years ago. Peter Legh was the eldest son of Richard Legh, of Lyme,Cheshire and succeeded to the large estates on the death of his father in August 1687. He was just eighteen years old when the great responsibility came to him, and though under age entered at once upon the duties of owner. He had married in the early part of the year, Frances, the only surviving daughter of Piers Legh of Bruche Hall, and by the marriage the Bruche estate became reunited with the patrimonial lands.

It was a critical time for the leading families of the land and the situation in which Mr. Legh found himself might have taxed the caution of a much older man. Like his father he was a follower of the Stuart Kings and after the abdication of James II and the " Peaceful Revolution," fell under suspicion of having hostile intentions towards the new Protestant king. Both he and his father had failed to attend the Coronation in 1685, a fact which was sufficient in itself to put them under sus- picion. Piers Legh of Bruche was present and wrote a letter ,to Richard describing the event and expressing his profound concern that all the Lyme family had treated the event with disregard.

===== 0002601_19090605_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHESHIRE CLUB.

Another fact '.which told • heavily against the youthful head' of Lyme was the know- ledge of his connection with ti..club of Cheshire gentlemen who Met for social purposes at each other's houses but who were under the suspicion of meeting to plot against the King. These gentlemen ten in number,have been interestingly described by the late Wilbraham Egerton, and portraits of them hang in the hall at Tatton. That, some were opposed to the King is certain, but that they met to plot his overthrow is • far from probable. But it was a time when feeling ran high. Suspicion was aroused by the discovery of arms in transit from London to Lancashire and Lord ,Delamere went to the length of mobilising troops on Bowden Downs as a warning to the Jacobites. The • Cheshire Club continued to meet and only realised when too late the grave risks :they were taking. A man named John Lunt made the statement that Peter Legh had accepted a post as Colonel in the service of the absent James. Lunt was a man of no character and a common informer, but, such was the tension of the period that his word was accepted. On the morning of July 19th, 1694; while Mr. Legh was dressing, a 'King's Messenger with Lunt and attended by fourteen Dutch troopers arrived at Lyme. The !men were armed and pushed their way to the bedroom to inform Mr. Legh that he was under arrest on a charge of treason. His papers were examined and a quantity of them packed up to be carried to London. The house was ransacked in search for arms but beyond a carbine and a case of pistols nothing was discovered. COMMITTED TO THE TOWER.

Mr. Legh with an escort of twelve blue- cloaked Dutch troopers was conducted to Knutsford and next day to Chester where he was closely imprisoned until September Ist. With five or six others he was then carried to London and appeared before the Duke of Shrewsbury on a charge of treason. The Tower was the state prison and the grim fortress received the supposed plotters to its cheerless cells on September 12th. For a week Mr. Legh was treated with severity and kept in solitary confinement. After this his wife was allowed to see him on condition of her sharing his imprisonment. A few days later by the intercession of the Queen he was allowed to take exercise on the Tower walls, but so rigorous were the conditions that when on one occasion his mother tried to speak to him the sentinel presented his musket and threatened to shoot her. After a month in the Tower he was called for trial and conducted to Chester Castle for this purpose. But the case entirely broke down. The informer, Lunt, was a miscreant of the most infamous type. He had been a highwayman, and had become a plot-hunter because the business promised to be profit- able. At the trial he made a sorry figure and the Crown solicitors were compelled to treat his evidence as unworthy of belief. That such a case should have been made upon the evidence of such a man is surprising and the story makes strange reading for us to-day, but it must be remembered that intrigues and con- spiracies were common. The country was in a state of ferment. Informers were paid for their services and plot-hunting became as gainful a trade among unscrupulous men as witch-finding had been nearly a century before. Peter Legh had not enjoyed eighteen months of liberty before he was again the victim of the plotters. On April 15th, 1696, he was arrested on a charge of having been engaged in Sir George Barclay's plot to assassinate the King and again was com- mitted to Chester Castle. A similar break- down in the evidence occurred and he was set at liberty. But these two bitter experiences remained fresh in his memory to the end of life. He was a sensitive man and the injustice pierced deep. Nearly fifty years after in some directions for his funeral he wrote, "I would have no monument set over me but a plain brass, nailed to the wall, to express my innocency in that wicked conspiracy by false witnesses, imprisonments and trials in 1694 and 1696, to convict me of high treason." Peter Legh died in January 1744 and was laid to rest at Winwick along with his wife and only son who had predeceased him nearly twenty years. Those who call at the old Church may see the brass" nailed to the wall" which records his innocence, and those who can gain access to Lyme Hall may see there a fine portrait of the much-slandered Peter Legh. Warringtonian have Trinity Church as his permanent memorial, and by this they will best remember him.

A. Me:

MR. WILLIAM BENNETT.

My father, the late William Bennett, commenced life at Lower Walton, and one or two incidents in his early life are worth recording. His parents died when he was quite young, leaving him destitute and penniless. The only legacy he received was my grandfather's dying wish that he was to be sure to be honest, a legacy which was, I believe, to him more priceless. than gold. A kind friend, Mrs. Barnes, the wife of a cattle dealer who kept the old Stag Inn at Lower Walton, was good enough to find a home for the lad, and no doubt thinking that he might become useful in the future, she provided him with the necessities of life in exchange for such services as he was able to render. Living at an inn, it sometimes happened that the customers would give him odd coppers for holding horses, &c., but he was not worth a money box, and as his funds increased, he was puzzled to know what to do with them. Fortunately, Mr. Barnes had land adjoining the old Tom Paine's Bridge, which, as many of your readers will ' remember, originally ran from Greenall's-avenite across the Old Quay Canal to Walton, just beyond the point at which Mr. Thomas May has found so many interesting Roman remains, but was demolished during the construction of the Ship Canal. In this bridge my father found a hollow place and, by inserting a brick, was able to keep his money here unnoticed and continued to do so for a considerable time, until his " Bank " contained, I believe, some .E2O or thereabouts. I have often heard him say how carefully he had to watch on all sides before depositing his coppers in this curious hiding-place. As time went on, he became extremely useful to Mr. Barnes, was taken to the different markets and fairs with him,and soon became an expert in the trade. He was often sent long journeys on foot, with great droves of cattle, sometimes as far as Shrews- bury, Newport, and similarly distant places. The great country fairs are gradually dying out, or being superseded by the weekly cattle markets, and the conditions of the trade are vastly different from what they were in my father's days. There were not any railways then, and he must have travelled many thousands of miles in this way before he was provided with a horse. He now began to think himself of some value to his employer and asked for a salary of fifty pounds a year; but Mr. Barnes objected, and it ended in my father starting business on his own account with a capital of perhaps a hundred pounds. He was already "courting"_ Miss Jane Swinton—to use the good, "old-fashioned word. She was the daughter of a respectable local family who lived at the Walnut Tree Farm, overlooking the old Mill Pond at Lower Walton. Eventually they were married, and spent a very busy, long and happy life together; and the writer has no hesitation in saying that my mother had a great and charming influence over her husband and family. She had a very kind and pleasing face and was a decidedly religious and pious woman, and the force of her example is felt by her descendants to this day. Her parents were Baptists and worshipped at Hill Cliffe ; and in the graveyard just above the chapel many of our ancestors have been interred. Some of them were Baptist preachers and officers in the days when the floors of this quaint old meet- ing-house were made of the bare earth and regularly strewn with dried rushes. Immediately after his marriage, my father took a place on Friars'-green, where he kept a few cows as a beginning; but here he was almost ruined, for, about this time, one of the most extraordinary floods on record oepurred 'in Warrington. I have heard my father say that boats were to be seen sailing to and fro in Bridge-street and the neighbour- hood, and so destructive was the flood that all the hay and other crops in Arpley Meadows and the surrounding country were either swept away by the stream or rendered quite unfit for use. Indeed, tge price of hay rose to the enormous sum of £lO or .£l2 per ton, which proved exceedingly disastrous to my father at the time. He removed to Black Brook (the home of Thomas Bird, an early local poet), and thence to the house adjoining thei•esidence of my son, Alderman Bennett, at Paddington, now known as Springfield; and here I was born, on the 21st of August, 1835, two years before Warrington Bridge was built and Queen Victoria ascended the Throne. Afterwards he moved to Pear Tree House, just beyond the present Padgate Station, and later still, to Tidy-lane Farm, where( he remained until his retirement. The business now began to grow rapidly; his four sons (of whom I was the third) gradually grew up and began to assist him; and eventually the firm of "Bennett s& Sons," or "Bennett Brothers" as it was called after my father's death, became known as that of one of the largest firms of Cattle Dealers in the North 'of England. It has been in existenee now for over eighty years, and although the business is carried on at the present time by a third generation, Tam vain enough to think that it has not lost the reputation which my father did so much to win. He died at Mount Cottage on' the 30th March, 1883, in :his seventy-eighth year, and it is not too much to say that he carried out his father's wish—that he was to be sure to be honest— to the end of his long and busy life. My mother had died some years before on the 17th of August, 1872. Two beautiful stained glass windows in Padgate Wesleyan Chapel commemorate their virtues, and of each it may be truly said in the words inscribed below my mother's window, that "The memory of the just is blessed." I have often wondered whether my father was in any way related to the John Bennett of Warburton, who was one of Wesley's preachers ,and married Grace Murray, with whom the great evangelist was said to be in love. The proximity of Warburton and

Walton and the comparative unfamiliarity of the name suggests that this is, at least, a possibility. In my early days I was sent to a school in Bank-street, near the present Ragged school. It was a large room over a stable, and is at present occupied by Messrs. Ashton and Crompton; and it is a curious coincidence that, for twenty years, my son's two papers, "Sunrise " and ' The Dawn," should have been printed in the very room in which both I and Mr. Crompton (so I understand) received our early education. The school was kept by an old soldier, Mr. Charles Nelson, who had a cork leg and no fingers on his right hand; but he was at that time cOnsidered one of the most beautiful penmen in the town. From there, I was sent to a school which was then known as one of the best boarding schools in the neighbourhood. It was kept by a Mr. Garlick at Cadishead. The education in those days was very different from what we have to-day. The principal subjects we were taught were reading, writing, arithmetic, grammar and geography. Writing was a speciality, and I often tell my son, Arthur, that, at 74 years of age (thanks to Mr. Garlick) I can write far better than he can. In those days,the Post- man from Warrington through Cadishead could be seen daily passing in a small carriage drawn by two dogs, and the best way of getting from Warrington to Manchester was either by the coach or by the swift packet. This was a long narrow boat drawn by two horses, which, with lads on their backs, galloped along in grand style from Warring- ton Bridge to Manchester, changing horses several times on the way. My education was completed at a _school kept by a Mr. Johnson, in the house at Fearnhead,formerly occupied by Mr. James Cropper, one of the great advocates of the abolition of slavery, and now the residence of Mr. James Mason. A tablet to Mr. Cropper's memory may still be seen in the entrance hall. I was married in 1862, to Miss Harriet Jackson, eldest daughter of Mr. John Jackson, of Moore. It may be interesting to note that one of her uncles, Mr. Joseph Jackson, of Knutsford, was the original of the character of" Mr. Johnson" in Mrs. Gaskell's charm- ing story, "Cranford." On her mother's side, my wife was descended from the Ocklestons, a family that is still well-known in Cheshire. My father, having retired from the business, had removed to Mount Cottage, and on my marriage, I went to live at the old farm in " Tidy " (or Tidal) Lane, as it is more correctly called, removing after some six years or so, to Bruche, where I was tenant for over forty years. I could say a good deal if time and space allowed, about the difficulties we had, to face as a firm, especially during the great cattle plague, when most of the cattle in this country were swept away by the dread disease; or later, when the foot and mouth disease,and afterwards, the pleuro pneumonia, became so rampant in this_country. I may add that, during the latter scourge, not less than 140 cattle. were slaughtered at Bruche, and that we had not a single cow left to supply milk for our domestic wants. Thanks to the laws now in existence, these diseases have been stamped out, and I sincerely hope that the next move by this or by some other Government will be to stamp out the almost equal scourge of tuberculosis, which in my opinion, is more difficult to deal with than any of the other diseases I have mentioned. During my residence at Bruche, for which I always had a great affection, I was very fond of farming; and, although I had a very busy life, with my three partners, in the cattle trade, I succeeded in obtaining the Royal Manchester, Liverpool and North , Lancashire Agricultural Society's Prize, in 1893, for "the best manhged mixed husbandry and stock farm of not less thanlso acres."

Although my father and mother were both Baptists, I was brought up as a Wes- leyan, and for many years attended the old Chapel. I can vividly remember the visits to my father of the famous" Squire Brooks," the wealthy Methodist evangelist. On one occasion he rode over to Padgate from Cadishead in a butcher's cart, with a motley company whom he had gathered there, and, when my father went into the house, he was rather surprised, to find the good squire help- ing all these uninvited guests to a substantial potato pie in the kitchen. Some of the early Methodists were decidedly unconventional, as may be gathered from this incident. I took an active part in building the beautiful new Wesleyan Chapel, which was opened in 1870, has since been twice enlarged, and is still much too small ; have been closely asspciated with its affairs from the com- mencement as well as with the general work of the Circuit, and have had the privilege of acting in practically every capacity opeh to a Wesleyan layman. In 1906, the members of the congregation at Padgate were good enough to prgsent me with an illuminated address and a silver rose bowl, to com- memorate my fiftieth year of continuous office in connection with the Church, a token of their kindness which I need scarcely say that I very highly prize. Padgate has greatly changed since the days of my youth. Mount Cottage (or Grange Mount, as it is now called) was then an old thatched farm, with a ditch between it and the road. The Methodist services were commenced in a cottage which formerly stood in the field immediately beyond it. ' The chapel was built, I believe, in 1829, and has since been converted into dwelling- houses. In the days when I first attended it was part of the duty of the chapel-keeper to periodically go round the building, during the services, to snuff the candles. In a field upon the right hand side of Padgate-lane, behind Bruche Hall, were several old thatched cottages, with an open ditch in front of them. There was, of course, no rail- way. There were more thatched cottages on the site of the present chapel; the road ran past the three houses now standing in the field beyond the railway to the Hand and Heart, an old " jerry " house or beer shop, at the end of Green-lane, which stood upon the site of Padgate Bridge and was the scehe of the annual Padgate Wakes. This !was a

_ great event in those days. Bull and bear baiting were freely indulged in, and I well remember an old man who lived in the village and was known as "Harry Bear" because he was said to have kept a bear for this purpose. The present Stocks Inn had not been built; the old wooden stocks stood just in front of its predecessor; and the Church and School were both erected somewhere about this time. Immediately beyond the latter, on the right hand side, was a stone "pinfold," in which to lodge stray cattle, and Padgate was separated from Fearnhead by a brook and bridge commonly known as the "Stakes Lache," whatever that may mean. Green-lane was all open, with ditches on either side, and three or four farm houses. The Industrial school, of course, had not been built. In the other direction, up Orford-lane and round by the old King and Queen to Gorsey- lane, there were large pits full of water, with one or two white cottages in the fields between Padgate-lane and an old farmhouse which occupied the site of the Cemetery and etood somewhere uear the preeent entrance

gates. Further on, where the Barracks now stands, there was a very lonely place, with large trees and deep open pits along the road- side. This place was supposed to be haunted. I was myself once awfully frightened there. I was taking milk on a donkey's back.to the town one dark winter's night, and, as I was passing quietly, along, I heard chains jingling, and, knowing the reputation of the place, if ever my hair stood on end it was then. However, fortunately, the next morning I.found out the " boggart," as they were called in those days. It turned out to be a donkey tethered by chains to its feet. I may here remark that I took the milk to a shop in Stony-lane (now Lythgoe's- lane, I think) which was kept by two aunts of the late famous sculptor, John Warrington Wood, whose parents and family I knew extremely well. It is pleasant to recall the grand old avenue of noble trees which led to Orford Hall, with an obelisk at the end of it erected in honour of John Ireland Blackburne, the famous botanist,and the splendid trees and shooting rqnge in Orford Park, a little past the tanneiy.

As I look back, how things keep cropping up that I had almost forgotten. There was the great flood in July (I just forget the year), when, from the Cemetery to Padding- ton, and right across to Thelwall, the country was one vast sea. The damage to the crops at Bruche alone amounted to about .E5OO. I remember the water running through Darlington Lodge, then the residence of the late Mr. William Robinson, and people had to be carried through it, both there and at Paddington. Floods were frequent before the Ship Canal was made, but are now a very rare occurrence. I vividly recall the great fire at Cockhedge Mill, which belonged to my friend and neighbour, Mr. Samuel Rigby, and how we watched it from the Bruche windows. I also recollect most vividly the two great fires at Bruche, which both occurred within six weeks of each other, in the middle of the winter. Happily, all the cattle but one, which was in a place we called the " Hospital," were got out safely, but that was slightly burnt. We were never able to find out how the fires originated, but, at the time, were disposed to attribute them to an incendiary. The buildings were completely gutted, but some of the main walls remained, and in one of them may be seen a well- cut stone which contains the initials "P. L." and the date " 1666," from which I conclude that they were originally built by Peirs Legh in the year of the Great Fire of London. Bruche itself is, of course, considerably older. A very ancient room may still be seen in the Hall, which wag used by Alr. Rigby as a library, and I remember Mr. Rigby filling in the moat which formerly stood behind the house among the fine old trees which still adorn it. A school was held in my youth, in the old yellow building which has since been used as a stable and coach-house. The school was kept by a Mr. Skelton, and had a good reputation. My youngest brother, Joseph, attended it, and I remember that three boys were once accidentally drowned in the moat. Before I bring my rambling reminiscences to a conclusion, I would like to say that I have been told by old inhabitants who have long since passed away, that a great portion of Padgate was originally waste land, and ,was crossed by foot-pads (or paths), with gates hero and there, to claim, I suppose, a right of pasturage by those who owned the cattle, &c., and this is said to have been the origin of the name—Pad-gate. Strange to say, in all parish documents, Padgate is called Poulton-with-Fearnhead, which legally Includes the three villages of Padgate, Fearnhead and Paddington. Why this anomaly should continue I am at a loss to know. When I was a member of the Parish Council, some years ago, I tried to bring about an alteration, but I suppose there are legal difficulties in the way, and so the absurdity remains; but it . does seem to me that, for postal and other purposes, the alteration would he an advantage, as I only know of one Padgate, whilst there are several Poultons. How things change I have been told, on good authority, that herons used to build down Tidal-lane, and I have myself seen hundreds of wild geese flying over to Risley Moss (which was then the haunt of vipers) to breed and feed at leisure. The pole-cat, too, was once familiar in the neighbourhood and I have frequently seen the kingfisher along the winding reaches of ,Black Brook. Warrington was then, of course, a small country town with, I suppose, less than half its present population, and when the King rides past the house where I was born and on through Warrington, he will find a place that is vastly different from the Warrington of my early days. But great as have been 'the changes in my lifetime, unless I am much mistaken, the next fifty years may see still greater. When next the King is good enough to visit Warrington, it may be on an aeroplane! But I must not prophesy, and will conclude by thanking God for a long life, a good wife and a happy family of six, every one of whom, as well as all our grand- children, has been spared to comfort us in our declining years.

THE KING RETURNS TO CYCLING.

The announcement tbat His Majesty the King has been ordered by his medical advises to resume cycling, which he-dropped a couple of years ago,has been widely commented upon, and people who believe in healthy outdoor exercise, have taken the king's case as an example to prove their theory. There is no doubt that gentle cycling exercise is extremely beneficial. The propulsion of a first class bicycle or tricycle is not a heavy muscular tax, and even a person who is almost an invalid can obtain great benefit from quiet riding on a cycle. The fact that His Majesty is again taking up cycling, will very likely be followed by a distinct revival of interest in the pastime amongst the upper classes; it is generally found that anything which our popular monarch does, is copied, first by the classes, and late, by the masses. King Edward possesses three Humber tricycles, and it was his habit to keep one at. Buckingham Palace, one at Sauddeghain, itud the thitO at Wlnclackr.

A sketch of summer sports, and especially a sketch of golf in the Engadine would scarcely be complete without at least a corner of that massive . pile of masonry, the " Kulm " in the foreground. Because if out- door sports have now spread from end to end of that spacious and smiling valley in the Southern Highlands, the " Kulm " has surely been the pioneer in all the development of summer, as indeed also of winter sports, that has taken place there. And golfers who by the hundred betake themselves to the Engadine in July and August (and even both earlier and later in the season) would indeed be lacking in, gratitude if they failed to recog- nise what has been due for many years past to the prescience of Herr Jansen and his never-failing encouragement to sport. So let us look for a moment at the busy 1 scene in front of the hospitable portals of the Kulm Hotel between nine and ten o'clock on a bright August morning. Just opposite the Hotel are the tennis courts already in great demand as they will be every hour of the day, particularly by those who are working up their game for the important Tournament which takes place on thew Courts every August. How many a famous battle royal has been fought out on these precincts where such eminent players as Mrs. Winch, Madame Neresheimer, Miss Brooksmith, Miss Lottie Dodd, Mlle. de FC•rplon, and Countess Palfi—to mention but a few of the ladies, and the ladies only, who have taken part in the St. Moritz Tournament—have for many a year past delighted the crowded audiences which season by season gather there. For in that gay and pleasure-loving world the Tennis Courts of the old " Kulm " are throughout the August Tournament the chief scene of attraction. , THE ST. MORITZ LINKS.

A stone's throw further on and we reach the golf links; a short 9 hole course, but in its way one of the most unique, bizarre, fascinating and thoroughly sporting little courses in all Europe. It is not possible in the limited space at my disposal to entex into any minute description of these links, but a word or two about the very first hole will show the thoroughly Alpine character of the golf. A full straight drive over one or two intermediate obstacles lands you, on a fair-way which cannot be more than twelve or fifteen yards across, though it is guarded by a bank on the left. If such a drive is sliced it takes you over a steep declivity amounting to something very like a precipice, and Heaven alone knows where you will get to ultimately. And the same awful hazard threatens a sliced approach shot on the right all the way up to the hole. Now if the pawky " golfer, in terror of the penalty that awaits a sliced shot, hugs the bank too closely and pulls his ball over it, he is again in equally impossible country on the left. There is nothing for it but the straight and narrow way, which is also a rather steep slope up to a small plateau on the crest of the hill where is the putting green. A good drive from the tee and a light iron or mashie will take you there, but your second shot must not be too •strong or it will rush violently down a steep place beyond the green, and that constitutes another formid- able hazard for any beginner to negotiate if he overplays his approach. I remember playing many years ago with a nov ice who made that mistake at this hole and descended into the depths beyond it. lie had about a score of shots in vain attempts to get back ; then he picked up his ball remarking that he preferred golf to racquets. From this breezy eminence one looks down into the exquisite mirror of the lake below, where are reflected in a thousand delicately shaded gradations of colour both the wooded hillsides and the mountain opposite. Towards the South East, across the well-wooded undulating valley that leads to Pontresina— that most famous mountaineering resort in this part of Switzerland—and rising immedi- ately above Pontresina is the picturesque and graceful, if hackneyed, Piz Languard. There is a wonderful view from flit Lawn ard extending from end to end of Switzerland, and even far away in the blue mountains— the Weinte Fern—of the Tyrol as well; while you are at close quarters with the mighty glaciers and snowy summits of the monarchs of the Bernina group which are right opposite.

, To return to the links and continue our brief survey of them, the chief features of these links are undoubtedly the iron and mashie shots, some of which require to be handled with great nicety and skill. Take the sth hole for instance; a very pretty one shot hole on to a well-guarded green which may be reached either by an iron approach or a fairly full mashie shot. Here again a ball that is badly overdriven gets into worse trouble than ever, because it descends into a hazard some hundreds of feet deep and is swept away at the bottom by the waters of the Inn, to reach,it may be presumed, ultim- ately the Danube and the Black Sea. Then the Bth hole—an iron shot on the lofty plateau—is quite unique in its way as is the famous hole by the sea at Biarritz, where the object is to reach—and stay on—an all too narrow ridge far above one. This entertAining little course is, as I have just said, a capital place for any one to cultivate the iron—that most artful and artistic of implements. There is any amount of variety, opportunities for lofted approaches and run-up shots ; for the perilous and infinitely scientific threequarter, with now and again the amusing and speculative ricochet into the bargain. And when you have had enough of this and want to open your shoulders a bit oftener, then take your clubs and yourself off to Samaden. a

GOLF AND GIiWtING IN THE ENGADINE.

SPECIALLY CONTRIBUTED BY MR. ft H. CROSFIELD, rit.p

over the massive foliage and venerable timber of some hoary denizen in a fine old English park. Then what is more sporting than that extra bit of the corner off the wood, taken at a dog-legged hole by your daring long driver. Or what more exciting than the low shooting stroke —not exactly the " push," stroke of which Mr. Charles Hutchings is the most famous exponent at golf, though no doubt the difficulty may be negotiated that way, but rather a jerky little shot where you stand in front of your ball with your iron club well" faced over,"—what more exciting than this kind of shot played beneath over- hanging boughs, by which an erratic drive may be repaired and the fair-way regained. True if beneath the trees there is tangled undergrowth, that is another proposition altogether. For there is nothing more irritating at golf than the vexatious delays caused by prolonged search for lost balls. [Although there may be a certain amount of hardihood in questioning the wisdom of the powers and authorities that be, I ven- ture to suggest in passing that the present rule about lost balls is just one of the two or three unfair survivals in the game that are still waiting to be remedied.] Fortunately there is no undergrowth of that kind at Samaden and the trees there, so far as they enter into the hazards of the course, are the greatest possible addition to them; and as is the case on so many other links they help to prove that far from trees being the worst kind of hazards, or as golfers once upon a time argued, no hazard at all, they may easily be one of the best. Which to be sure only shows that error may be as prolific and prejudice die as hard on a golf links as anywhere else. Well, so much for the hazards on the course. The lengths of the holes is well judged, the object of those who have laid out and developed the links being to give the long driver his chance at a goodly proportion of full two shot holes, without however by any means letting him have a monopoly in that respect. And then the turf! And the putting greens! The mountain scenery! And

content unless they pit themselves against the laws of gravitation, even with the odds of 6 to 4 in favour of the latter. Still if you do your work yourself, without any ignominious haul at the hemp, you will find that the Drei-Blumen are not child's play, and that a few hours devoted to these most appropriately named and picturesque rocky pinnacles is by no means time wasted. More ambitious spirits will find plenty of other work on a larger scale a little further afield; for instance the Piz Rosegg, one of the loveliest, the most superb mountains in all Switzerland ; theCrast ' Aguzza (this sounds like some blood curdling Italian oath; but it isn't; it is only the name of a mountain, though to be sure a mountain which is rather a terror, especially in anything like bad weather) not to mention other equally and still more formidable undertakings. The two mountains I have just named are hidden from view by some of the other giants of the Bernina group. Of these there is a very fine view from the Samaden links, and one of them, the Piz Palu, is here reproduced. It is remarkable for the rare beauty of its snow, and the evanescent and magical beauty of the colour of its ice. Moreover, its outlines are uncommon and have a certain ,beauty of their own which would have delighted Ruskin. A still more imposing mountain, and a very fine peak too when one gets to close quarters with it, is the Bernina itself, the highest mountain of the group, but this is for the most part hidden from the links, and it is beyond my present purpose to attempt to enumerate all the snowy and rocky summits which invite the most ardent golfer to forsake his liege lady and take a day 'off for climbing now and then. Time was when one would have thought twice about even mentioning mountaineering in the same breath as golfing. It would have given too good a chance in those days to the scornftil sportsmen who spoke disdainfully of" shinty stick" as a game only to be taken up wl-en cricket and tennis—and indeed quite a variety of other games—had long since been discontinued. But that was twenty years ago and the. Philistines who used to revile

the prince of out door games have learned the error of their ways and are silent ; nay, save a few hardened sinners, they are converts, and those who used *to scoff have learned to play. I have not left myself space to describe other golf links in the Engadine, either those already in working order, or those in pro- cess of development; I can only in a single sentence allude to the beautifully situated course at Maloja, which again like the links at St. Moritz, has a special character and special features peculiarly its own. At Maloja as elsewhere on the Continent, innumerable golfers owe a permanent debt of gratitude to the experience, judgment and tireless energy of Colonel Woodward, formerly associated with golf on the Riviera, and who of late years has done so much to further the progress of the Royal and Ancient game in Switzerland.

THE SAMADEN LINKS.

The Samaden course extends over a broad stretch of level ground—a plain that is formed of the alluvial deposit washed down for centuries by the glacial streams that feed the waters of the River Inn. If links like these at first sight appear dull, that is but another illustration of the deceitfulness of appearances, because from the first tee shot to the last put there is not a single tame hole. Some tiny tributaries and one good sized brook wind in and out their devious channels through the links. There are also a few dried up water courses, dnd traces of the remains left behind by some ancient moraine. These and some other natural hollows that have been converted into hazards, with a few artificial bunkers thrown in here and there, and certain groups of pine trees, make up the difficulties and dangers of the course.

Now there used to be in the minds of golf- ing experts, or most of them, a rooted anti- pathy to trees. How this antipathy arose I know not, still less why it has lasted so long. For why should any golfer be prejudiced against the " Tufted thicket and the leaf strewn hollow." What prettier sight can he wish for than to pee his ball soaring with graceful trajectory
