1E PIZ PALU IN THE BERNINA (1 IZOIJP.

mountain air! Who that has the chance to take himself and his clubs to the spacious and invigorating uplands of Samaden or Montana woulcl remain through the dog days in The sweltering heat of the lowlands plodding there round parched links under a broiling sun? True, Old Sol pats one genially on the back even in the Engadine, but his attentions are never irksome and unwelcome, for through the summer months there are cool breezes more delicious arid refreshing than the

" Blest sherbet sublimed with snow " itself.

While in August golfers North and, South of the Channel are generally complaining of that baked earth and parched turf which are ,fatal to fine iron play, up at Samaden there is ever a wealth of grass—and yet not too much of it: tempting lies for the brassey throughout the whole course, and such exquisite mountain turf on the putting greens as is surely surpassed nowhere else. This is the result of a good deal more than the natural qualities of the grass, because for the last twelve or fourteen years Dr. Patterson has given devoted and unremitting attention both to the putting greens and t& every part of the links, which as a result are now in the very forefront of the best links on the Continent.

THE MOUNTAIN SCENERY. As reference has been made to the moun- tain scenery, perhaps a word or two about this in conclusion would not be out of place; for certainly some of the views on the Samaden links are very striking. In one direction is the fine rocky outline of the Piz Julier, which with its glacier and precipice looks down on the Pass 'where two thousand years ago the Roman Legions crossed the Alps. Nearer at hand are three interesting and very pretty little rocky minarets called the Drei-Blumen, and any one who cares to coMbine mountaineering with his golf will find these well worth attention. What the character of the climbing there is may be gathered from the accompanying illustra- tion. WARRINGTON TO HANKOW.

WEST TO EAST OVERLAND.

BY GEORGE REEVES.

(Continued from last week.)

I fail to see any difference between the artizan class of Russia and England, or even the business or commercial class. Their cus- toms are similar to ours, only they suffer to a greater extent than we do from high taxation, landlordism and political disabilities. I noticed the poverty problem is an acute one in Russia, and a large number of beggars frequent the streets. If begging can be called freedom, then the poor of Moscow possess one freedom, for begging is not written down as a crime. I may mention here that freedom is not only reduced to an infinitesimal quantity in respect to Russian subjects, but to foreigners travelling through Russia. An experience of inine will prove my contention. Upon arrival at Moscow statioh a military-looking gentleman approached me, asking in broken English (and very much broken indeed for I had a difficulty in understanding him) if an English ' lady had travelled in the train. I answered "No." He then left the station. I drove to the Hotel Metropole, and I had just seen my juggage to my rooms, when I i ' was asked for my passport. I thought t had I been pronounced enough, so I impatiently asked who wanted it. I was directed to a 'gentleman standing behind me, who was no other than the military gentleman who accosted me at the station. I learned he was of the police spy fraternity and the question about the lady on the station was simply a subterfuge to find out my nationality. My passport was returned to me exactly one hour before leaving Moscow and I had to pay 2 roubles 20 kopecks for the police com- missioner's stamp thereon. I had now accomplished three days' travelling covering 1801 miles, and the journey had by no means been monotonous, though I had not met a single English- man en route, so far. I boarded my train Sunday midnight, on the Trans-Siberian Railway, the only other occupant sharing my berth being a Russian Army officer, who knew not a word of English. This damped my ardour somewhat, as I now had a train ride for nine days without a change, any- how. I indulged in sweet repose, and as the day dawned I found myself on the Siberian side of the Ural Mountains, in Asiatic Russia, and entering upon an immense low- land plain, extending towards the Polar Sea. I glanced at my map, and found I was only 8 degrees south of the Arctic circle. The surrounding country was a desert of snow, with no signs of life, I either man or beast, except occasional gangs of men clearing the snow from the railway track, and these men looked more like animals than humans, for they were clothed from head to foot with skins, only their eyes being exposed. In some places along the track, the snow had been thrown up by these gangs to such an extent that the train would travel a mile through a gorge of snow higher than the train, obscuring from view the surrounding country. As the train drew more east, the country became more fertile, and the monotony a the scene was broken by the appearance of villages, half buried in snow, and farmsteads scattered here and-there. Now and again a forest of pine trees mingled with silver birch appeared, occasionally the tinkle of sleigh bells could be heard, and squads of sleighs, drawn by long-haired ponies, could be seen gliding along, over the glassy surface of the hard trodden snow tracks. Up to now my experience on the Siberian run was not encouraging and raised no great hopes of. anything different, until I had crossed Siberia. My Russian fellow traveller seemed bent upon acquiring my tongue, or at least sonic of it, and by the use of German, French Russian, English, and signs, gesticulations and •so forth, he learned a' little 'English, and I a little Russian. When we were tired ' of this kind of thing, I re-read my stock of literature and truly carried out the maxim, " read, mark, learn etc." but I could not learn to comfort myself on this part of the journey. If I stepped out of the train at a station to exercise my stiffened limbs, I encountered a keen, biting, icy blast from the Arctic regions, which froze the moisture on my moustache into a block of ice. This can only be expected, if you face an atmosphere of 135 deg. of frost, and such was the case in Siberia. The days dragged on, until the seventh day I arrived at Irkutsk, a Siberian city on Lake Baikal, one of the finest fresh water lakes in the world. Lying in the valley of a mountainous district, this lake freezes over several feet thick in winter, and travellers through Siberia, early in 1908, had to cross by sleighs, as no railway existed round the lake, as at present. Two days later the train arrived at Kharbin, I had now crossed Siberia, having covered more than 6,000 miles in nine days, in one train, I ought to mention the trains are de Luxe; I cannot conceive of any finer trains for comfort, in existence. They are fitted with electricity throughout, good sleeping berths and draw- ing rooms, lavatories, etc., and a fine restaurant, with a good kitchen. You can walk the whole length of the. tfain. and special attention is given to the heating arrangements, an even temperature being kept up night and day. Conductor-guards and attendants each perform their duties well, and worthy of praise. My stay in Kharbin only lasted 2!„ hours, so I did not venture outside the station, owing to the extreme cold. I next entrained for Quanchen. tz. e, on the imperial Chinese rail- way;. arriving at the above place the same evening. I had rather a rough time here, as I had to stay the night over, sleeping on two tables, in a one story hut called an hotel. Next morning at five o'clock, I drove 2.1 miles through rugged country, in a vehicle like an old-fashioned perambu- lator and a very extreme frost, to the South Manchurian Railway, belonging to the Japanese. I then entrained for Muckden, the capital of Manchuria, staying there over night, owing to no train connections. Muckden is a very old city, surrounded by two great walls having very ancient Chinese houses and temples. I left Muckden early

next morning, again taking the Imperial Chinese Railway, for Tientsin. I arrived at the latter place after a journey of 24 hours. During the journey a striking comparison in,. scenery presented itself. Not a flake 131 snow could be seen, neither had there been any, and the hill slopes were bedecked with the bright green bamboo trees, sheltering beneath them the artistic rice beds and tea gardens. Stretching away as far as the eYe, could see were farmsteads, their lana parcelled out with long lines of green tuft and every field under cultivation. I felt as. if I had jumped clean out of winter int° summer, the contrast between Russia and China was so striking. Tientsin is a fine city—in the neighbourhood of the foreign Legations. These are well laid out under sanitary conditions, but after I had visited the Chinese city and the scene of the Boxer War I was quite satisfied, and left the citY early next morning. A dirtier place than the Chinese city in Tientsin I never saw in my life. My next run was to Peking, the capital of China. A journey of four hourg brought me within the city walls. There are more than 4,000 walled cities in China, and they but little differ from one another, except in size. Peking possesses a huge wall all round the city, with here and there a many 'storied tower called a, Pagoda, which like a sentinel marks the side of each of the 16 great gates. The Pagodas present a pretty sight, being covered with different coloured tiles. Mounting one of these pagodas, one can see an almost continuous procession of springless carts, called the Pekin cart, wheelbarrows and sedan chairs, coolies carrying their baskets balanced on a piece of Bafnboo across the shoulders, merchants hawking their wares,and heavilY laden camels carrying tea to Mongolia. There are really three cities within the walls, the Tartar or Manchu city to the north, the Chinese city to the south, and the forbidden city in which the Emperor lives (of course the Emperor was dead, and lying in state, the time I visited Peking). I had a good vieW of the interior of the forbidden city, from the top of a Pagoda, by the American Legation. Probably it is well known by now that the forbidden city was entered by Germans, Japanese, French, and English soldiers, during the Boxer rising of 1900, and photo- graphs of the yellow and green tiled palaces were taken by German officers. What is known as the Kaiser-street, is the finest Chinese street in Peking for space, but is verY dirty. Generally speaking the streets are very narrow and dirty, containing deep ruts, and in wet weather the carts plough through them up to the axles. There are no pave- ments, and progress through such streets i 5 very difficult and obnoxious. The only open spaces I could find were in front of the Temples and Yamens (Judges' Courts). 10 the north-eastern side of the city the verY old Temples are to, be seen, such as the Temple of the Moon, the Sun, Agriculture, and so on. The Emperor is supposed to visit these Temples and observe certain peculiar and ancient customs every year. One special feature is the roasting of an ox, sacrifice to the gods (but I must leave for a future article the religious observances of the Chinese). Credit is due to the attractive and, excellent way the foreign Legations are laid out. Fine stately buildings, standing in spacious grounds, the roofs peering through a mass of foliage amongst winding gravel paths, reminding one of the line "East to West, home is best." My stay in Peking lasted two days, and I ought to mention (without any attempt at advertisement) the Wagon de Lits Hotel, where I stayed, is the 'finest, in my opinion, in the East, and much credit is due to Pekin for raising such a fine structure of Western architecture. My next run would bring me to my destination. This occupied two days, arriving in Hankow about three o'clock the second day. This is one of the finest treaty parts on China's largest and most dangerous river, the Yangtse Kiang (Great River) 600 miles inland from Shangai, ships being able to reach this port direct from Liverpool in certain months of the year. I profited much in many ways from m3r journey both mentally and physically, as I learned something and gained almost a stone in weight, but I would advise the "Examiner" readers to take the trip in summer-time and acquire some knowledge of six languages before starting.

WALKING DAY,

TOWN COUNCIL RECANTS.

Original Date Decided Upon.

The many protests sent in from all quarters against the arbitrary action of the Town Council in altering the date of Walking Day from Friday July 2nd to Monday July sth, wjt.hout consulting the partiet; most closely concenied with the arrangements for the Sunday School festival, have had the desired effect. A special meeting of the General Purposes Com- mittee which consists of the whole of the members of the Council, was held at the Town Hall on TuesdaY afternoon to re-consider the Matter. The Mayor pre- sided over a good attendance After 801ne diSCUSSIOII it was decided to revert to the old arrangement and Walking Day will, therefore, be held on Friday, July 2nd.

The Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle) has Since issued an official notice `` That having regard to representations made to the Council, that arrange- ments have been completed on behalf of many of the Sunday Schools and Works for the 2nd and 3rd July, which it has been found cannot now be altered; it has been decided that the procession of Sunday scholars shell take place as originally fixed, ,on Friday, the 2nd July next. The Rector had convened a meeting of SundaY School authorities to be held at the National Schoolfk on Wednesday evening but in view of the Council's decision, it was cancelled.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

INTERNATIONAL ORDER OF GOOD TEMPLARS. The Good Ternplars of Earlestown held a social on Whit-Monday evening in their Lodge-room, Legh- street, which was thoroughly enjoyed by all the member's and visitors. The tables were nicely arranged by a number of the Sisters, who served refreshment. Musical selections were given by Mr. 11. Stretch, of St Helens. Mrs. E. Willis sang "tThe Echo Boy," which was well rendered. An address was delivered by Mr. E. Newman on the 'benefits of being a Good Templar. Be pointed out how many reople bad gained positions in life throligh the instruction taught in Temple and Lodge. During the many years' work of our Order in the Army we bad planted 02 military branches of , the Order, of which 50 were located in Vales, England, Scot land, Aden, Sicnapore and China; two each In Ireland. Hong Kongand the West Indies; three each ,in Gil,ralter and Ceylon ; five in Egypt, six in Malta and fourteen in South Africa. some of the men had gone front Farlestown and neighbourhood, and it was a pleasure to bear from them how the Order was increasing. Mr. J. Warburton gave a I.rinceshire reading, " Ben Seddon. Miss Prichard, St. Hejens, g.ave a reclr9l. A hearty vote of thanks to those who had arranged the social brought the meeting to an end.

LABOUR BUREAU.

During May, 159 fresh apPlirants registered their names for employment, being 47 men, 39 3 ouths, 47 womsn, 26 girls. Of the 97 men registered, 20 were married, two were widowers and 25 single, 23 w ere skilled workmen. and 24 unskilled; 11 of the women were married, 13 widows and 23 single. Thirty three fresh situations were ofierei by employers, and the number of situations filled through the liureu during the month was 43, ot whom 20 were for men, nine youths, ten women and four girls. 'Mete are now on the register out of employment 247 persons in the following trades :—Building trade, 6; iron and wire trade, 36; horsemen, 4 ; clerks, 6; general labourers, 38; miscellaneous .occupations, 18 ; youths under 18 years old, 57 ; women for (glaring and daily work, 48; domestic servants, 2 ; girls under. 18 years Old, 32. Employment in May appeared to show a slight improvement compared with the previous month. There was a seasonal improvement in the building trade and outside workers generally which to some extent reduces the amount of unemployment, but on the other hand the iron (rale. appeared to remain dull, much short time still being worked. The number of fresh applicants for employment was not so great as during the winter mouths and they were in. a great me9.sure confined to the iron and wire workers. _ . _ .

Many woman continue to make use of the Labour Bureaus in the hope of obtaining employment at chairing and daily work, the inljorkly of whom are wives of iron and wire workers who owing to their husbands being out of employment or on short time are anxious to do something to assist in keeping the home together. Amongst them are many experienced char- women who aresuitable for almost any class of domestic work. and ladies requiring char women can be well suited at the Labour Bureau.

THE CALL OF THE COUNTRY.

(A Plea for the Garden Suburb Movement.) When you may dwell in a garden why should you live

in a slum ? Why should you toil in the sweltering town when the birds are calling come? When the grass is green in the meadows and the daisies golden-eyed, Come, for the birds are calling; come tO the country side!

Come from the crowded alleys where you scarce can see the sky, For the shade of the tall brick bv;ld;ngs and the smoke-clouds rolling by; Come from the squalid pavements and the courts where the children pine; Come from the haunts of the fever to the fields where the cowslips shine ! Come, for the cuckoo calls you; come, where the bluebells grow; Where the happy hedges whiten Avith the hawthmn's scented snow ; Come, where the brooklet gargles ; come where the corncrake cries; Come to the woodbine hedges under the sunlit skies !

Oh, friends, w:11 ye hug your fetters when we seek to set you free? Would 3on rather have the slimy kerb than the throstle's minstrelsy ? Is the sight of your ash-tubs sweeter than the scent of the new-mown hay Is the gutter the place for a flower-faced child in our English land to play ?' Are you happy under your chimneys, amid the reek and the roar? Would you sooner have the flaring forge than the roses at your doo r? Is thiofilthy pavement dearer than the daisy-spangled sod ; And the breath of your back-yards sweeter than the unveiled face of God?

We will build you homes to dwell in, and places wherein to play You shall delve in your fruitrol gardens at the close of the busy day ; You shall twine the blue clematis rovnd your windows and your walls ; And your rest shall be sweet, in the svrnmer heat. when the purple twilight falls. And here you shall bring up children whose faces are fair to see, Whose limbs are sttong and whose hearts are glad with a new-found liberty; And the filth and the smoke and the squalor shall slowly pass away, And the night grow bright in the rosy light of the dawning day ! ' ARTHUR BENNETT.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

There is the usual varied entertainment at the Hippodrome this week, though some of the turns might be classed as " ordinary " and not very , attractive. The Bank Holiday audiences were very large and, appreciative, but the bill did not sustain very full houses for the' rest of the week. Bella and Bijou are the star performers, and having been here before with very much the same repertoire they run a' risk of palling on the taste of a generous public. The Suffragette sketch is good, but it is losing its freshness. The Bros. Ashley strike out a novel line with their farcical sketch entitled " Next Please," the fun all taking place in a barber's shop and, ending with a very pretty "scrap" between the tow'h rough and one of the customers for a shave. The boxing is of the lightning variety and greatly entertains the crowd. Some capital dancinc, both in solo and concerted •numbers is provided by the Society Quartette, one of whom recalls recolledions of the .inimitable Little Tich by his cleverness in the big boots. Fred Ward, comedian, has plenty of fen in his composition and a most expansive smile on the outside. He provokes plenty of laughter. Sira, the one-legged acrobat, per- forms some remarkable feats, and other turns of the light variety are given by Toff Noel and Daisy Hurdle. The pictures are excellent, and the band selections capably played.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME

For next week the management announce the important engagement and 2,500 th performance of Ernest Cadle's screamingly funny act " Casey's Court," by the Midget Mimes, thirty in number. The sketch represents a street urchin's idea of producing a variety entertainment, including midget comedians, actors, ventriloquists, 'illusionists, &c. Will Murray will appear as Mrs. Casey. The great Albas, the marvellous wire performer, will present his original and remarkable feat, sliding on his head down a slanting wire. The other artistes will be —J. H. Hawtrey, actor vocalist ; Mark Milton, ,eccentric comedian; Mdlle. De Rose, the Lady Foli, assisted by Miss Hilda Claremont, the charming girl soprano ; Bros. Connolly, comedians ; Ernesto, juggler and musical eqUilibrist, introducing his latest ladder novelty. -

WARRINGTON'S HEALTH.

MEDICAL OFFICER'S ANNUAL REPORT.

The Paving of Back Passages.

Dr. J. Coote Hiblort, medical officer of health for Warrington, has just issued his annual report on the sanitary condition of Warrington, and he deals in an exhaustive and interesting way with the outstanding features of the public health of the borough during the past year. We give the following extiacis

===== 0002601_19090605_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
VITAL STATISTICS.

The death... Sate from all causes for the year was 17 0 and the zymotic death-rat) 2.6 pr 1120 population; while the infantile death-rate per 1,000 births was 134. These figures show a slight increase on those for the preceding year, which were respectively MI, 2.1 and 122. %%herr compared with the average of those for the 76 great towns of England and Wales, the Warrington rates (and especially the zymotic rate), are seen to be somewhat in excess of these averages. The only special disease which accounted to any extent for the higher death-rates during the year was whooping cough, 60 deaths having been attributed to this disease as com- pared with it during 1907. There has also b: 2n a slight increase in the nurnier of deaths doe to enteric fever, influenza and diarrhoea; on the other hand a decrease in the number from measles is recorded. The higher irfairtile death-rate is almost entirely accounted for by the increased number of deaths among infents from whooping cough. As regards the notifirible infectious diseases, the main points to be noted are the continued prevalence of scarlet feVer in a mild form, the deflnite increase in tie number, of cases of diptheria, and the occurrent e of a small outbreak of enteric fever in the early part of the year. In connection with the non-notifiable infectious diseases, it is a somewhat startling fact that the two diseases' whooping 'cough and measles, which are often d: rned but trivial ailments, accouuted for 86 deaths, while the three, notifiable diseases, scarlet fever, htberia and entaric fever, together caused only 43 deaths.

===== 0002601_19090605_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
TUBERCULOSIS

has again claimed during the sear a larger number of ‘ictiles than any other disease, 141 dcaths, or about of the total nuruf er of deaths from all eauses, having been attributed to this rualtdy. In my report for 1907 the sources from Which infection is detived were dealt with and the desirability of making two of Sankey Sanatorium for the treatment of early cases was sug- gesteil. Except for the admissic•n of a few cases of Pulmonary TutereulosiS into a hut in ihe grounds of the Aikin-street Isolation Hospital during the summer month's, the only special effort to deal with the dist ese in the Borough has been by obtaining the voluntary notification of cases. Such patients are subsequently visitea and advice is given on the need for cleanliness and ventilation of the home, and on the ptecautiors necessary to ptevent the spread of infection to others ; spit-bottles and disinfectants are supplied and the rooms occupied by the patients, together with bedding Szc., are disinfected when occasion arises. Eighty eight cases were voluntarily notified during the tear- In connection with tuberculcsis it is interesting to note that the most cc :ent report issued by the Royal Commission investigating tee subjezt hss supplied further proof that the milk from a tuberculous cow may contain tubercle bacilli without there being any sign of disme in the udder. This is a very importsut point as the chief powers which most sanitary authorities possess with respect to tuberculous cows are limited at present to the dealing with animals which have diseased udders. Many of the present insanitary cowsheds must be thoroughly infected with tubercle bacilli given off by genetatiens of tuberculous cows,, and cons: luently milk sent out from such premises may hecerne infected with the bacilli even after it has left the cow. As most of the milk supplied to Warrington comes from dairy farms situated otitside the borough, the dirc :A dealing with this question rests for the most part with other authorities, but it is none the less an importsnt matter to milk. :tonsumers in the bciroueb. • The retsil of milk on the small scale by tumorous vendors front premises quite unrstisfactory for the pr.rpose has formed the subject of a report. to tl e committee, and, although the dealing with the batter has ben deferred until the provisions of the promised Milk Bill are known, it is to be hoped that the sale of this food; which is so easily infected, and which forms so important a part of the diet of infants and invalids, will soon he put under such restrictions as will tend to ensure its freedom from contamination. GENERAL SANITARY CONDITION OF THE BOROUGH.

In my report for 1907 I laid great stress on the importance of the expeditious and systematic removal of all animal and vegetable refuse frem the tow.), and especially from the poor and more populous districts. The more infirm:lll 1-nowledge of the: Borough I have obtained during.the past twelve months compels me again to point to the need of more energetic action in this direction. It would be useless, tor me to suggest at the present time the conversion of the pail-closet system of excreta removal to the far more sanitary ater-oloset system; but,so long as the present method of dealing with excreta exists, the least that should Le done is to see that the pails are removed with such frequency as will ensure that no unnecessary nuisance is caused.

During the year the Chief Inspector and myself have devoted considerable attention to the COURTS AND BACK PASSAGES, and it is in these poorer arees that the defc Ms of the pail-closet system are chiefly to be seen. From one cause or another, whether from the pails being overfull or from their incorrect position or from some defective structure of the closet seat, liquid filth in the majority of instances is to be seen flowing from the pail place on to the adjacent part of the yard ; and, to make the matter worse, the yard- surface on to which the filth flows is nearly always either unpaved or imperfectly paved, and is also, except in one or two instances, undrained. Hence this ground, which adjoins the dwellings and which is of;:en the playground of the children, becomes soaked with undiluted sewage. The dealing wit i this question together with that of the satisfactory scavenging Of these areas, is at pr t sent under comideration, and it is in this direcit ion that our main efforts must be • applied in order to reduc) the death-rat 3 from the preventible diseases, i.e., the zymotic dienses, including infantile diarrhoea. In this connection I must again rater' to those large and badly constaucted micidenst3ads attached to stables, slaughter-houses and cowsheds, whicr are still existing. These structures must be replaced by properly con- structed receptacles' which should not accommodate more than a week's manure. A considerable amount of insanitary property has been dealt with during the year under the Housing of the Working Classes Acts. It having been decided that the present time is not rm. opportune one for the building of a public abattoir, certain sanitary improvements are being obtained with respect to the

PRIVATE SLAUGHTER HoUSES IN THE,BOROUGH ; but unfortunately many of these existing buildings, on account of their position and structure, are incapable or being transformed into premises which are suitable for the slaughtering of animals. So long as the sanitary supervision of slaughterhouses is not uniformly exer- cised throughout the country, it will be difficult to meet one of the chief arguments at present put forward against the building of a public abattoir in any town namely, that as soon as a municipality begins to put special restrictions on the slaughtering of animals within its boundaries which are not imposed by neigh- bouring districts, butchers will do their slaughtering just beyond the municipal boundaries, and that in consequence the municipal tibattoir, if erected, will be a financial failure. • BACTERIOLOGICAL WORK.

Dr. Hibbert lays great stress upon the value of bacteriological examination in order to adequately control such infections diseases as Diptberia, Tukier- culosis and Enteric Fever. But, in order that full benefit may be derived from this brr ich of public health work, the laboratory must he close at hand so that unnecessary delay is avoided in investigating any case ; and as this work is greatly increasing in amount, it is also essential that the laboratory he a part of the Health Department, otherwise the expense involved in sending numerous specimens for examination at an independent institution would become a serious item and would tend to cripple work*

===== 0002601_19090605_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
UNCERTIFIES DEATHS.

The number of uncertified deaths among infants, i.e, deaths of infants as regards which neither a doctor's certificate has been given nor tan inquest held, is again excessive. The proportion of uncertified deaths to total deaths which occurred. in the Borough during the year was 4.4 per cent, while the proportion for England and Wales as a whole was I'4 per cent. This fact has been commented upon by the " Lancet " en savetal occasions during the rast twelve months, and the question was asked why the paportion was specially high in Warrington and a few other towns. As far as lam aware the provision of medicid aid for the poor in Warrington compares favourably with that made in other tom and Otero should therefore be no difficulty in obtaining a doctor's attendance in any case of illness. There is, moreover, no reason for believing that rs course to such help is exceptionally delayed in this town. The only explanation, theiefore, that I have to offer for the relatively large number of uncettified deaths in Warrington is that inquests are held less frequegly in this Borough than in the country gener- ally on cases where a doctok's certificate cannot he obtained. I must again point out that it is very desir- able that an inquest be held in connection with deaths of young children when a doctor has not been called in during the fatal illness. EXEMPTION FROM VACCINATION.

It is especially important that the effect of the amendment of the Vaccination Act, passed in the latter part of 1907, be brought before the public notice. As the result of this modified legislation 93 children during the past 12 months have been refused by their parents the protection uhich is undoubtedly offered by vaccina; tion against the dire risks of an attack of smallpox.

INFANTILE MORTALITY.

The number of deaths of infants under one year Per 1,000 births was 134 during 1908, as compared with 122 for ate preceding year. Tbe higher figure for the 3 ear under report is entirely accounted for by the increased number of deaths from whooping cough. Except during the month of August, the summer of 1908 was by.no means a hot or dry one, and the meteorologic.' condi- tions which prevailed during the summer and early autumn were not very dis-similar from thote vWch during 1907 had contributed to the comparatively low infantile death-rate for that 3 ear. An infantile mortality rite which is over 100 must, however, even for a town such as Warrington, he consioered excessive, and the figure for 1908 will be exceeded during future years with hot summers, unless the paving and scaveng- ing of the poorer districts are more rigorously attended to; As the time of the lady inspector is chiefly taken up in supervising the work of the midyt ives, no systematic visiting of new-horn children can he attempted. Further action in this direction, such as ae appoint- ment of a second lady health visitor, and the adoption of the Notification of Births Act, is undoubtedly desirable.

PAVING.

The paving of new streets is often dflayed fora Icng time, while in hack par.sagps in this rev; t, the condi- tion of things is even worse, but it is now to be hoped that a decisive improvement will he made. TYPHOID. FEVER.

In a special report on the outbreak of typhoid fever early in the ear, Dr. Hibbert says the source of infec- tion has not been. traced. No fin ther evidence has been obtained connecting the Parr Hall with the cause of the ou,tbreak, and, as a matter of fact, although some of the patients were at the dance at the hall on December l3tb, the onset of the symptoms in these cases, about December 20th (except in one instance), was too soon after the dance to point to that occasion as the time of the infection. The general sanitary arrangements of the hall were, found to be satisfactory, and samples of' water taken from various taps on the premises, and submitted to bacteriological examina- tion, were found to give no evidence of contamination. The water supplied to the Borough for domestic pur- poses has been shown by regular bacteriological and chemical analysis to be above suspicion.

Dr. Hibhek gives a warning ;gainst the use of the Appleton water, supplied for trade uses only, for drinking purposes.

MR.. SEDDON,.M.P., AND COMMERCIAL TRAVELLERS.

At the Commercial Travellers' Conference at Northampron on Tuesday, a letter from Mr. Seddon, M.P., was read stating that he would include com- mercial travellers in his Bill against illegal agreements. The Conference declared in favour of Government or county authorities acquiring canals in view of the action of railway companies in making working arrangements. A resolution approving the Daylight Saying Bill caused opposition, being carried by 66 to 53. Delegates called for appointment of commercial men as our Consnls in foreign countries,

LYIYIII MAY QUEEN

situated on a pleasant upland and •commands a fine rural prospect, was the scene of the Coronation festivities and when the procession arrived there, the enclosure was well lined with spectators. With quaint and elaborate ceremonial the Queen was escorted to the throne and the diadem was placed on her head by the crown bearer. The pre- sentation of the sceptre completed the cere- mony and loyal subjects cheered heartily. The Rose Queen was seated on the left of "her Majesty," and maids of honour, beefeaters and courtiers grouped themselves in picturesque fashion about the throne. Then the revels began. A troupe of little children tripped across the greenward to engage in that'indis- pensable feature of May celebrations—the Maypole dance—and right cleverly did they perform their parts. Swiftly were the varie-Soloured ribbons entwined about the pole and as readily were they unplaited. Thedance reflected great credit upon Miss Daniels who was responsible for the training of the children. The ancient was soon coupled up to the modern, for Mr. F. Brabrier,a Manchester avnateur,next gave a skilful exhibi- tion of roller skating. His imitations of the blundering novice were keenly relished and great was the amuse- ment when he made an undignified exit at the behest of a very small policeman who literally swept him off the platform with a broom. Miss F. Jackson, of Knuts- ford, attired in Welsh costume, gave a fancy dance with a daintiness which earned many a round of applause. For one so young she is surprisingly expert. The members ot the Oughtrington Gymnastic Club gave evidence of their agility in a gymnastic display of great merit, under the leadership of Mr. T. Leigh. A fairy dance, for which a bevy of little lasses had been trained by Miss Spinks, of Warrington, was gracefully executed, and seldom has a prettier spectacle been seen at these festivals. The Irwell Springs Band gave a masterly interpretation of the " Tancredi "overture and there were loud cries of "encore," but time forbade a response. The Golliwog dance was an orignal item, the curious yet pleasing movements being admirably done. Miss Sjiinks had also trained the performers for this item as well as for the fascinating Eastern dance in which the brilliantly costumed children presented a picture of rich colour end animation. Master. J. Gidmen and Miss Dean, of Knutsford. danced the favourite Irish jig so admirably as to call forth applause from all parts of the field and the lively Hornpipe dance by the Misses Dorothy Dean and Bessie Jackson was equally appre- ciated. The music for the various dances was supplied by the Irwell Springs and Lyn= Subscription Bands. The committee had offered prizes for the best trades- men's turnouts and there were about eight entries. Mr. Wood, of Altrineham, made the awards as follows Fred Ingham, Heatley ; 2, J. B. Egerton, Lymm ; 3, William Rowbottom, Agden ; 4, Charles Gleave, Broom Edge. In the absence of Mr. E. Pickavance. the judg- ing of the cyclists' section was undertaken by Messrs. J. Harvey, A. Booth and W. Chalmers, of Warrington.

The winners were :-1, Arthur Saville (Carrie Nation) ; 2, Messrs. Ball and Miss (Dam family). At the close of the afternoon ceremony the committee entertained about 500 childran at tea in the Lymm schools, kindly placed at their disposal by the Rector (Rev. G. B. hurst on). Mr. and Mrs. Hirst had charge of the catering which gave every satisfaction.

In the evening there was another large audience, and part of the above programme was repeated with success. From 7.30 to 9.15 dancing was indulged in to the strains of the Irwell Springs and Lymm Subscription Bands, and the proceedings were voted a huge success. The festival was worked by a splendid committee, under the chairmanship of Mr. James D. B. Smith, with Mr. A. H. Fray as vice-chairman.f Mr. George Within was an energetic hon. treasurer, and be bad the assist- ance of Mr. IL H. Crotobed, who had the arrangements on the field in hand. Mr. T. Nash rendered a4mirable service in the capacity of M.C. Miss M. Warburton, the President of the Ladies' Committee, was untiring in her efforts to promote the success of the undertaking, land she was ably seconded by Miss H. Brundrett as hon. secretary. Mr. J. Webb, jinn., was another leading worker, and at the rehearsals he played the pianoforte accompaniments to the dances. Mrs. Lord kindly placed a number of old English oak chairs at the disposal of the Committee for the crowning ceremony. Finally, a meed of praise must be accorded to Messrs. V. W. , Ashton and H. S. Saville, junr., by whom the secretarial work was most cheerfully and efficiently done.—A balance sheet will be published in due course.

FESTIVAL.

PICTURESQUE CEREMONY.

Lymm is an ideal place for a May Queen Festival. The quaint, winding streets .and leafy lanes of the picturesque village give the old-time pageantry setting that would be hard indeed to excel, investing it with that idealistic touch which constitutes half its charm. And on Thursday, Lymm—always beautiful— was looking at her very best. The countryside was gay with the rich laburnum and delicate lilac, and fragrant with the perfume of red, and white hawthorn blossom, while on every hand a wealth of foliage met the eye. The good people of Lymm crown their May Queen in June, but this delightful disregard of tradition is generally justified by events. As a rule there is less likelihood of a cold and cheerless day for the great festival in June than at the opening of the " merrie month" I. another advantage is that Manchester always keeps holiday in Whit week and sends its quota to swell the throng, and last but by no means least, Lymm wears her loveliest natural garb. In the early part of the year the fate of the festival, which is now well established, was hanging in the balance. The financial-responsibilities of the undertaking are con- siderable, and it was 'realized that some special effort was necessary unless Lymm was content to see its festival become merely a pleasant memory and not an annual reality. Fortunately the Lymra Tradesmen's Association. was able to enlist a gratifying measure of support from the inhabitants and at a public meeting a • large and repre- sentative committee ,was 'formed. The members have been untiring in their exertions and subscrip- tions to the amount of £44 have been collected, while the arrangements were so complete as almost to exclude the possibility of a hitch in the programme. The expenses are approximately estimated at 3.:,150, so it will be seen that a great deal depended upon the backing which the promoters received from the

visitorWattracted froni surrounding districts

On Thursday the old-world i-ranquility of the village was for the nonce dispelled by the invasion of thousands of people from Manchester, Warring- ton and other places. By rail, cycle and waggonette they streamed in, until the streets were

A GROUP OF OFFICIALS.

Mn. F. W. Amu); (Hon. Sec.).

well nigh impassable. The day broke beautifully no but towards noon clouds appeared and many anxious glances were directed upwards,for rain would have been a dire calamity. Insurance against the elements, though a praiseworthy precaution, does not atone for bedraggled dresses, discomfort and even danger to the little ones. To the joy of everyone the sun broke through—after a struggle—and although the outlook occasionally was unpromising, brightness at length prevailed and the pageant and revels were carried through without any interference. By 1.30 the ancient weather-beaten Cross—the pride of Lynun —was the centre of a scene of animation, for it was here that the procession was to start on its way. On all sides was a sea of faces and the massive base of the Cross itself became an excellot place of vantage for as many spectators as could &ore or less comfort- ably find room on it. Martial strains were heard and soon the famous Irwell Springs Brass Band—the champion band of England—which the Committee had engaged to lead the procession, .made its appear- ance. Picturesque indeed was the spectacle as the pageant unfolded itself. The first section vas a cycle parade but the entries were disappointing.. However, the wierd and wonderful " make-up " of the competitors, most of whom attempted something an- the comic line, afforded great merriment. Carrie Nation, of saloon smashing notorietv,was amongst the notabilities impersonated, and cast menacing glances around, as if in search of a crusading ground. A couple of down-at-the-heel individuals—apparently of the Weary Willie and Tired Tim family—took turns on an ancient tricycle and displayed a notice " On • the road to Rostherne," which seemed to tickle the onlookers. There was also a cobbler with brawny anus and a well-padded chest, who pur- sued his trade with the. enthusiasm of a village blacksmith. Following the cyclists came a series of lurry tableaux, which formed a highly attrac- tive feature. Britannia, with helmet and trident was a dignified figure, and a New Zealander was to be seen offering her a Dreadnought. The model ship, we hear, had, actually come from New Zealand and had been lent by. Mrs. Bennett. There were pleasing representations of the seasons, a charming fairy queen, a bevy of May girls, a merry gipsy seated by the camp lire, Grace Darling, the heroic sailor lass, and the" Old woman in a shoe," who on this occasion found little difficulty in keeping her large family under control. Then came several hurry loads of "England's Hopes "—smiling youngsters who seemed to be enjoying their shate in the great event Immensely. The mounted characters added another touch of variety and colour to the pageant, and right well they comported themselves on their steeds. The Duke of Wellington viewed Napoleon with the utmost amiability and not many paces behind rode Lord Roberts. Cavaliers and highwaymen fraternized and a huntress and huntsman looked quite striking in their scarlet array. Then a medley of characters on foot passed before the delighted spectators. A group of dusky gentlemen revelled in "The Darkies' May Festival "and althongh the " Queen " tried to assume a mock dignity the grotesque aspect easily predomin- ated. The Lymm Subscription Band headed a large detachment of boys and girls representing England and her Colonies, the characteristic costumes of each land presenting an ever-changing and brilliant picture. Troupes of fancy dancers tripped gaily along, and the boys of the Oughtrington Gymnastic Club marched with soldierly step. A host of characters familiar in nursery rhyme ; pierrettes, garland girls and Geisha girls, an amusing batch of "Golliwogs and Teddy bears" all followed in rapid succession. There was a murmur of admiration when a little maid rode past in a very diminutive carriage drawn by a goat. It was, of course, inevitable that the suffragettes should be impersonated, and one demonstrative member of the "sisterhood displayed a card with a variation of the familiar motto. It read "Blokes for Women." Preceded ,by beef- eaters and courtiers came an open carriage con- taining the Rose Queen (Miss Ada Jones, who was the May Queen last year) attended by pages and maids of honour and carrying a handsome bouquet of roses and maidenhair fern, presented by Mrs. Hancock. Court ladies, crown and sceptre bearers, and six maids of honour followed, and last of all in another carriage rode the May Queen (Miss Emma Dixon, daughter of Mr. Charles Dixon, of Statham). :` Her Majesty "looked the picture of queenly dignity and she smilingly acknowledged the acclaims of the multitude. She wore a beautiful Empire dress of ivory silk trimmed with Valenciennes lace and her bouquet,- presented by Mr. S. Davies, was composed of lilies and carnations. All the maids of honour carried choice bouquets. As the long line of colour wound its way through the thronged streets, nothing but praise was heard, and there was a consensus of opinion that the procession, in which about 500 children irrespective of denomina- tion took part, excelled any of its predecessors in aumbera and beauty. SUCCESSFUL WARRINGTON CHOIRS.

NOTABLE PERFORMANCES IN WALES.

Some notable victories were achieved by Warrington choirs in Welsh musical contests on Whit Monday. At the Bwlychgwyn Eisteddfod the Apollo Male Voice Choir, under the conductorship of Mr. Harry Berrey, won the first prize(l2o and a silver crown for the conductor) in the male voice choir contest. The test piece was "The Crusaders" and the Apollo choir defeated the famous Welsh choirs, Rhos and Brymbo. The adjudicators were Mr. E. D. Lloyd, of Cardiff, and Mr. Llewellyn, of London. At the Hawarden festival in the afternoon, the Apollo choir were awarded the first prize (.1.5 and a massive silver challenge cup presented by the Gladstone family). The adjudicator, Professor 'JOhn Henry, of Liverpool, spoke very eulogistically of their interpretation of " Crossing the Plains," observing that the choir was highly trained and was a pleasure to listen to. Never in his experience had he beard-a better rendering of the prayer movement. The Apollo Choir secured 95 marks put of the maximum of 'lOO, Liverpool Cecilia being second with 85. The other competing choirs were Hindley Co-operative, Connah's Quay and Birkenhead. At the same festival, the Stockton Heath Choral Union (also conducted by Mr. Berrey) carried off the prize (S2O and a massive silver rose bowl to be held for a year) in the chief choral competition. The test piece was "He, watching over Israel" and the adjudicator in awarding Stockton Heath 83 marks out of a maximum of 85, said he could conscientiously state that the piece was admirably sung, the tone and blend being beautiful. The contraltos were particularly fine and he "fad never heard a better blend of altos. It is a note- worthy fact that Stockton Heath, a very young choir, succeeded in defeating the Stretford Glee and

-CROgiIELD'S BAND AT SHREWSBURY

At the Shrewsbury Musical Festival on Monday, Perfection Soap Works Band won both the open brass band and quickstep competitions, with Foden Band second. The test piece in the chief contest was a selection from Verdi's works which has also been chosen for the New Brighton Festival where the Perfection Band will compete to-day (Saturday).

Clark —Worsley,

The marriage was solemnized at St. Oswald's Church, Winwick, on Wednesday of Mr. Charles Henry Clark, Ivy Bank, Newton-le-Willows, son of the late Mr. and Mrs. George Clark, and Miss Mary Olivia Frances Worsley, The Cottage, Winwick, daughter of the late Mr. James E. Worsley. The officiating clergy were the Revs. Canon Penrhyn, A. Inglis, and W. S. Knowles. The service was choral, the hymn" The voice that breathed o'er Eden" being sung. Mr. Helsby presided at the organ and played Mendelssohn's Wedding March. The bride who was given away by her brother (Mr. Edward G. P. Worsley) looked charming in a pearl satin dress trimmed with silk embroidery forming the shape of a horse shoe. She wore a wreath of orange blossom,a veil of old Brussels lace, and an antique pearl brooch. Her bouquet was composed of white heather, lilies of the valley, white roses and stephanotis. The bridesmaids were the Misses Helena J. B. Worsley, Paulina A. S. Worsley, Katharine A. J. Worsley (sisters of the bride) and Miss Constance A. Clark (sister of the bridegrom). They wore white eolienne dresses, with pink sashes, and carried bouquets of pink roses and lilies. Their ornaments were gold brooches set with turquoises and pearls, the gifts of the bridegroom. Mr. Ernest E. Woods, of Warrington, discharged the duties of best man. After the ceremony, a reception was held at "The Cottage," a large number of relatives attending. The bride and bridegroom left for London en route for Devon, where the honeymoon will be srent. The bride's travelling costume was of wedgwood blue soie-de-Lyon, with crush .strawoerry hat, and travelling coat of Shantung silk. LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Connor—Silcock.

A very quiet wedding was solemnized at the Church of Our Lady, Latchford, on Monday when Mr. Edward Arthur Connor, eldest son of Mr. Edward Connor, of Warrington and Southport, was married to Miss Margaret Silcock, daughter of Mrs. R. H. Silcock, Heber House, • Grappenhall. The Rev. Father Mars officiated. Mr. Harry Evans performed the duties of best man. Laser in the day the happy couple left for North Wales where the honeymoon being spent.

===== 0002601_19090605_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
• Silcock—Greenough.

There was a pretty wedding at St. Paul's Church on Tuesday afternoon, when Mr. Reginald Nicholls Silcock, Heber House, Stockton Heath, son of the late Mr. Reginald Heber Silcock, was married to Miss Mabel Dorothy Greenough, daughter of Mr. H. F. Greenough, 27, Bold-street. The bride and bride- groom are both well known and highly esteemed, and a large 'congregation assembled to witness the ceremony. The officiating clergymen were the Rev. H. V. Pigot and the Rev. W. Bracecamp. The service was fully choral and included the hymn" How welcome was the call." Mr. a Wilkes was at the organ and played choice selections, including Mendels- sohn's "- Wedding March." The bride, who was given away by her father, was charmingly attired in a dress of ivory satin mousseline. The bodice was trimmed with ivory ninon, with silver edging and pearl drops. The bridal veil was of white tulle, surmounted with orange blossom, and she carried a handsome shower bouquet. The bridesmaids were Miss Elsie Greenough (sister of the bride) and Miss Molly Silcock (sister of the bridegroom), and their dresses were of mauve chiffon taffeta, trimmed with Irish crochet lace and buttons. They carried shower bouquets of sweet peas. As the bridal party left the church the bells rang out a joyous peal. A reception was held at the Masonic Rooms, Bold-street, and later the newly-married couple left Warrington for London and the Channel Isles. The bride travelled in an Empire dress of antelope (re jasper) satin charmeuse, with trimmings of 'aluminium on corsage and draped belt of turquoise blue, with a three-quarter semi fitting coat of the same material, and hat to match. Many handsome presents were received. The bridegroom's present to the bride was a set of furs, and the bride's gift to the bridegroom was an easy chair.

Buchan— Forster. The marriage of Miss Nellie Forster, only daughter of Alderman J. Forster, J.P., ex-Mayor of St. Helens, and Consul-General for China, with Dr. John James Buchan, the borough medical officer,was celebrated on Thursday afternoon at 'Wesley Church, St. Helens, in the presence of a very large gathering of friends and relatives. The bridegroom has filled the post of medical officer with especial ability for the past five years. He is the eldest son of Dr. Buchan, of Cleland, Lanarkshire. A day or two too Dr. Buchan was the recipient of a handsome entre(i'dish and silver cake basket from the doctors of the town •, the staff of the medical officer's department gave him a silver coffee pet, and the nurses and staff of the Sanatorium a silver inkstand. The bride and her family have been closely associated With the affair's of the town, her father having been a member of the Corporation for twenty-five years: Close on twelve months ago he received the important appointment of Chinese Consul- General for Great Britain, and among the bride's presents was a valuable piece of old Chinese silk embroidery from the Chinese Ambassador, Lord Li Ching Fong. The ceremony was conducted by the Rev. David Jones, of Harrogate, and formerly of St. Helens, assisted ' by the Rev. William Collett, superintendent minister. The church was decorated with flowers, and the service was choral. Mr. E. Heywood, F.R.C.0., organist of the Parish Church, rendered selections of bridal music before and after the ceremony, The bride wore a Princess robe of ivory satin, with a panel of . crystal net in the front of the skirt, and the bodice was trimmed with crystal embroidery. She carried a sheath of lilies of the , valley. •The brides- maids, Miss Madge Gallie, of Southport, and Miss Tripp, of London, were also dressed in ivory satin, with boleros of guipure lace, transparent yokes Of net, and short sleeves of .guipure, black picture hats, and carried bouquets of shaded sweet peas. The bride was given away by her father. The best man was . Dr. George F. Buchan, of Hounslow, London, and the bride's four brothers, 'Messrs. Walter Forster, Willum A. Forster, J. Stanley Forster, and Arthur C. Forster, acted as groomstnen. A reception was held at Wynne House; where 120 guests were invited. The bride's going-away costume Was a pale

Madrigal Society, the holders of the Warrington Musical,Festival Shield, who obtained 78 marks. The Buckley Musical Society was placed third. The mem- bers of the choirs and 'their gifted conductor are to be congratulated upon their achievements which have afforded great satisfaction and pride to the towns- people generally. WARRINGTON MALE VOICE CHOIR AT LLANWRST.

At the Llanwrst Eisteddfod on Whit-Monday the principal .event was the competition for male voice choirs, of which five had entered. The test piece was " On the Ramparts," and was sung in English. The choirs sang in the following order :—Warrington, (conductor) Mr. E. B. North), Penmaenmawr (con- ductor, Mr. Christmas Jones), Wigan Harmonic (conductor, Mr. A. Bullock), Moelwyn, Blaenau Festiniog (conductor, Mr. Cadwaladr Davies), and Cilgwyn (conductor, Mr. R. W. Roberts). The piece was admirably sung by all the choirs, but it was con- sidered that Penmaenmawr, Wigan, and Moelwyn gave a dramatic rendering, which lifted their per- formance above that of the other choirs. Mr. David Evans (Cardiff) and Mr. John Williams (Carnarvon), the adjudicators, awarded the first prize of £3O to 'Moelwyn, and the second prize of £lO to Penmaenmawr.

Ray—Darlington.

In the presence of a large congregation the marriage took place at St. Mary's Church, Great Sankey, on Whit-Monday, of Mr. *James Ray, Sycamore Bank, Sankey, scin of the late Mr. John Ray, and Miss Florence A. Darlington, daughter of Mr. Thomas Darlington, Heath-road, Penketh. The Rev. J. 11. Jones (Vicar of Sankey) officiated. The service was fully choral, the hymns sung being " Lead us heavenly Father lead us " and "The voice that _ ..

breathe a o'er Eden." The bride, who waS. given away by Mr. William Davenport, wore a beautiful Princess robe of white silk voile, with hat to match, trimmed with ostrich feathers. Her bouquet was composed of lilies of the valley, The bridesmaids were Miss Victoria Davenport and Miss Maggie Darlington (sister of the bride) who were tastefully attired in white embroidered muslin dresses with floral sashes, and Leghorn hats trimmed with pink flowers and tulle. They carried bouquets of pink gladiolas and clematis, the gifts of the bridegroom, and their orna- ments were gold brooches, the gifts of the bride. Mr. David Ray acted as best man. Miss Standish presided at the organ. The reception was held at the Sankey Sunday school, many relatives and friends being present. Numerous handsome presents were received, including a set of silver carvers and forks from the staff of St. James' school, and a handsome clock and silver jam spoon from teachers at Sankey Sunday school and members of the Sankey Choral Society. The bribegyoom's present to the bride was a case of cutlery.

===== 0002601_19090605_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
Mercer—Booth.

A marriage of considerable local interest took'place at Sion Chapel, Newfield View, Lymm,un Monday, when Mr. R. H. Mercer, son of, Mr. and Mrs.- W. Mercer, Cherry-lane, Lymm. was married to Miss Edith Booth, daughter Of Mr, and Mrs. Thomas Booth. Cherry-lane, Lynam. A large congregation assembled and the hymns "The Voice that breathed o'er Eden" and "How Welcome was the call" were sung. Mr. T. Worthington, of Wigan, an old friend of the bride- groom's parents, performed the ceremony, and Mr. H. T. Mercer was at the orgat. The bride, .who was given ' away by her brother (Mr. John Booth, of Altrincham), was charmingly attired in heliotrope, trimmed with 'chiffon and lace and ,carried a bouquet of white roses and smilax. Her ornament was a diamond and ruby brooch, the gift of the bridegroom. Miss F. Mercer (sister of the bridegroom) was the bridesmaid, and she wore an . Empire gown of cream crepe de chine. Her bouquet was composed of pink and white carnations and her ornament was a gold and steel pearl brooch, the gift of the bridegroom. Mr. Harry Mercer .acted as hest man: After the ceremony a reception was held at The Institute, Booth's Hill, Lynn, and later the happy couple' left for Blackpool, where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was a blue tailor made costume with hat to match. The bridegroom, who is secretary of the Independent Methodist Sunday School, was presented by the teachers and scholars with a hand- some Westminster chiming clock. MUNICIPAL UNDERTAKINGS. -- Profits on Gas, Water and Electricity.

The Secretary of the Gas Department has presented a Balance Sheet for the year ending 31st March last, showing a net profit of £13,387 7s. 2d., which will be carried to the credit of the Improvement Rate. The Borough Treasurer reports that for the year end- ing 31st March. 1909, the gross profit on the Water Undertaking amounted to £19,596. and after paying out of this the Sinking Fund and Interest Charges amounting to £18,500, there remains a net profit on the year's working of £1,091 Bs. 4d., as against a loss of £1,047 4s. sd. for the previous year. The net profit has been kept in hand to increase the working balance, which has been considerably reduced during the past five years. • DECREASE IN TRAMWAY AND ELECTRICAL

===== 0002601_19090605_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
RECEIPTS.

The report ot the Borough Treasurer with regard to the profits on the Electricity and Tramways Under- takings for the year ended 31st March, 1909, was read to the Committee on May 18th. The Treasurer reports that for the year ended 31st March last, the gross profit on the Tramways Undertaking amounts to £8,124 Bs. 11d., as' against £8,316 Os. 4d. for the preceding year. The Sinking Fund and Interest charges have been met out of this, leaving a net prat of £2,370 6s. 88., as against last year's profit of £2,579 lie. id. This sum bas been carried to the Reserve Fund, which now amounts to £6,480 3s. 10d. The passenger receipts have been £19,320 Is. 6d. or 13.620 d. per car mile, as compared with £19,782 93. 7d. or 10827 d. per car mile for the preceding year. This diminution of £462 in the receipts is the first check to the steady increase which has hitherto annually occurred in the income of the undertaking, but it may be regarded as only temporary and mainly due to the state of trade in the town. He also reports that for the same period the gross profit on the electricity undertaking amounts to £6,739 10s. id., as against £7,533 9s. sd. for the year ending ,31st March, 1908, and that after paying the loan charges, amounting to £5,796 17s. 5d., there remains a profit of £942 12. 6d., as against £2,213 10s. 6d. for the previous year. The income from sale of current has decreased by £1,153, and the amount received for meter rentals has also decreased by £l4O owing to the abolition of rents, but the work done, &c., for customers has realized £571, which is an increase of £219. The working expenses show a decrease of £382, but the loan charges have increased by £477 on account of the further loans taken up. The profit of £942 has been transferred to the Reserve Fund, which now amounts to £B,lOB Os. id.

--_ HOLIDAY MAKERS ON THE RIVER: BOULTER'S LOCK ON WHIT-MONDAY. - The Whitsun Bank Holiday was spent out of doors. The sun shone brilliantly all hay, and thc temperature in London-131 degrees—was the highest of the year. Thousands flocked to the parks and the seaside, and 18,000 went up the river to Hampton Court alone. Our illustration shows the scene at Bounces Lock on Monday, when the river was full of punts, rowing boats, steam pinnaces, motor boats, and pleasure craft Of every description. It was a glorious holiday, and assuredly one of the best places in which to spend it was on the cushions of a punt in a shady backwater on the Thames.
